\left\{\ket{\psi^{1}_{i}}\ket{\psi^{2}_{j}}\right\}
such that
|\lambda_{i}^{1}-1/2|>\gamma_{1}-2\epsilon
and
|\lambda^{2}_{j}-1/2|>\gamma_{2}-2\epsilon
.

(3)

The running time of the algorithm is polynomial in the running time of
P_{1},P_{2}
,
{1}/{\epsilon}
and
\log(1/\delta)
.

State Repair.

We state the state-repairing results (Lemma 4.9 and Lemma 4.10) from
[
CMSZ21
]
below to be used for achieving persistence. We adapt the formulation by
[
BBK22
]
with some additional modifications: unlike
[
BBK22
]
, we need some additional structural properties of value estimation and state repair procedures of
[
CMSZ21
]
formalized in the third bullet below.

Lemma 10
.

There exist efficient quantum algorithms
\mathsf{ValEst}
and
\mathsf{Repair}
with the following syntax and guarantees:

•

\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\rho},1^{1/\varepsilon})
takes as input the description of a verifier algorithm
V:\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}\to\left\{0,1\right\}
, a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, a quantum state
\mathbf{\rho}
, and an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon
. It outputs a quantum state
\mathbf{\rho}^{*}
and a value
p^{*}\in[0,1]
.

•

\mathsf{Repair}_{V,\mathcal{A},\Pi}(\mathbf{\sigma},y,p,1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{k})
takes as input a verifier algorithm
V
, a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, a
k
-outcome quantum measurement
\Pi
with outcomes
\mathcal{Y}
, an outcome
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, a value
p\in[0,1]
, and an accuracy parameter
\varepsilon
. It outputs a quantum state
\mathbf{\sigma}^{*}
.

1.

Value Estimation:

\displaystyle\E_{(\mathbf{\rho}^{*},p^{*})\leftarrow\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\rho},1^{1/\varepsilon})}\left[p^{*}\right]=\Pr\left[V(y;r)=1\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\\
y\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{\rho},r)\end{subarray}\right].

(3)

2.

Almost-Projective Estimation:
For any
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|p^{*}-p^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{\rho}^{*},p^{*})\leftarrow\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\rho},1^{1/\varepsilon})\\
(\mathbf{\rho}^{**},p^{**})\leftarrow\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\rho}^{*},1^{1/\varepsilon^{\prime}})\end{subarray}\right]\leq\varepsilon.

3.

2-Projection Implementation:
For every
(V,\mathcal{A},\varepsilon)
, there exist projective measurements
\mathcal{M}_{0}=(\Pi_{A},I-\Pi_{A})
and
\mathcal{M}_{1}=(\Pi_{B},I-\Pi_{B})
and classical deterministic algorithms
f,g
such that the execution of
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\rho},1^{1/\varepsilon})
does the following:

(a)

Initialize an empty database
L=\emptyset
.

(b)

Initialize an auxiliary register as
\ket{\psi_{0}}
, so that the current mixed state is
\rho\otimes\ket{\psi_{0}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{0}}
.

(c)

For
1\leq i\leq\mathsf{poly}(|V|,|\mathcal{A}|,1/\varepsilon)
:

•

Compute
b=f(i,L)\in\{0,1,\bot\}

•

If
b\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, apply
\mathcal{M}_{b}
and obtain outcome
\ell_{i}
. Set
L=L\sqcup\{\ell_{i}\}
, where
\sqcup
denotes disjoint union.

•

If
b=\bot
, end the loop. Output the current residual state
\rho^{*}
and the value
p^{*}=g(L)
.

Furthermore, the measurements satisfy the following:

•

\mathcal{M}_{0}(\rho^{\prime})
can be described as:

–

Pick
r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}
.

–

Compute
y\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\rho^{\prime},r)
coherently.

–

Measure if
V(y;r)=1
.

–

Uncompute.

•

\Pi_{B}=I\otimes\ket{\psi_{0}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{0}}
.

4.

State Repair:
For any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|p^{*}-p^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{\rho}^{*},p^{*})\leftarrow\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\rho},1^{1/\varepsilon})\\
(\mathbf{\sigma},y)\leftarrow\Pi(\mathbf{\rho}^{*})\\
\mathbf{\sigma}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Repair}_{V,\mathcal{A},\Pi}(\mathbf{\sigma},y,p,1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{k})\\
(\mathbf{\rho}^{**},p^{**})\leftarrow\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\mathbf{\sigma}^{*},1^{1/\varepsilon})\end{subarray}\right]\leq\varepsilon.

(4)

3.4
Unlearnable Distributions

Definition 1

(Unlearnability)
.

A distribution
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}(\lambda)
is called
unlearnable
if for any query-bounded adversary
\mathcal{A}^{P_{y}(\cdot)}
with oracle access to
P_{y}(\cdot)
, we have:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y^{\prime}=y:\begin{subarray}{c}y\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\\
y^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{P_{y}(\cdot)}(1^{\lambda})\end{subarray}\right]\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

4
Cloning Games - Definitions

We would like to capture all cryptographic games where the adversary needs to clone a particular functionality of a given quantum token. The quantum token could be a copy-protected program, signature token, unclonable ciphertext, unclonable decryption key, or any quantum state that serves some functionality which could only be used by one party at a given time. We start off with basic definitions and give generalizations in
Section

4.4
.

Definition 2

(Cloning Game)
.

A
cloning game
consists of a tuple of efficient algorithms
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver})
:

•

Key Generation:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
is a PPT algorithm which takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
in unary. It outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. We will assume without loss of generality
16
16

16

This is in order to simplify the notation for the rest of the algorithms. We will sometimes make this inclusion explicit, and other times it is understood implicitly.
that
\mathsf{sk}
always contains the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
.

•

Token Generation:

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
is a QPT algorithm that takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. It outputs a quantum token
\rho
.

•

Challenge Generation:

\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m
. It outputs a classical challenge
\mathsf{ch}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.

•

Verification:

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
, a message
m
, a challenge
\mathsf{ch}
, and an answer
\mathsf{ans}
. It outputs either
0
(reject) or
1
(accept).

4.1
Correctness

Before we talk about cloning experiments, we should specify what property of a quantum token
\rho
we would like to be unclonable. Intuitively, the property will be captured by the ability to honestly pass verification using the token
\rho
. This brings us to the definition of correctness for a cloning game:

Definition 3

(Correctness)
.

Let
\delta:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to[0,1]
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
has
\delta
-correctness if there exists an efficient quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
such that for all messages
m\in\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})=1\end{subarray}\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{ans}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})\end{subarray}\right]\geq\delta(\lambda)

If
\delta=1
(or
\delta(\lambda)=1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
), we say
\mathcal{G}
has
perfect
(or
statistical
) correctness. In this work, we will mainly focus on statistically correct cloning games.

Note: In the correctness definition above,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
should be considered an honest user of the primitive.

4.2
Special Types of Cloning Games

Next, we define some special cases, with terminology borrowed from classical security notions.

Definition 4

(Cloning Search Game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning game such that
\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})
accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}=m
. Then,
\mathcal{G}
is called a
cloning search game
.

Definition 5

(Cloning Decision Game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning game such that the answer
\mathsf{ans}
taken as input by
\mathsf{Ver}
is one bit, i.e.
\mathsf{ans}\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. Then,
\mathcal{G}
is called a
cloning decision game
.

We additionally define the notion of a
cloning encryption game
when we discuss unclonable encryption in
Section

4.5.2
.

4.3
Security

Cloning Experiment.

We will define notions of security for a cloning game in terms of a security experiment. Given a token
\rho
, an adversary should not be able to generate two (possibly entangled) quantum tokens which can simultaneously pass verification. We will formalize this intuition below.

Definition 6

(Cloning Experiment)
.

A
cloning experiment
, denoted by
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
, is a security game played between a referee
\mathsf{Ref}
and a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
. It is parameterized by a cloning game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over the message space
\mathcal{M}
. The experiment is described as follows:

•

Setup Phase:

–

All parties get a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples a message
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\rho
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

Splitting Phase:

–

\mathcal{A}
computes a bipartite state
\rho^{\prime}
over registers
B,C
.

–

\mathcal{A}
sends
\rho^{\prime}[B]
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\rho^{\prime}[C]
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Challenge Phase:

–

\mathsf{Ref}
independently samples
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

–

\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
send back answers
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes bits
b_{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}})
and
b_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}})
.

–

The outcome of the game is denoted by
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))
, which equals 1 if
b_{\mathcal{B}}=b_{\mathcal{C}}=1
, indicating that the adversary has won, and 0 otherwise, indicating that the adversary has lost.

Trivial Success.

As a baseline for unclonable security, we will consider
trivial
attacks that do not require any cloning operation. The best we can hope is that such attacks are optimal, hence the definitions below.

Definition 7

(Trivial Cloning Attack)
.

We say that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
is a
trivial cloning attack
against a cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
if
\mathcal{A}
upon receiving a token
\rho
, sends the product state
\ket{\bot}\!\!\bra{\bot}\otimes\rho
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
. In other words, only
\mathcal{C}
gets the token
\rho
. We denote by
\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
the set of trivial attacks against
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
.

Remark 1
.

Note that due to the symmetry between
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, the definition of trivial cloning attack could be equivalently defined so that only
\mathcal{B}
gets the token
\rho
.

Definition 8

(Trivial Success Probability for Cloning Games)
.

We define the trivial success probability of a cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}):=\sup_{\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)\in\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})}\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right].

Unclonable Security.

We present the security definition of cloning games below.

Definition 9

(Unclonable Security)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a cloning game,
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over the message space
\mathcal{M}
, and
\varepsilon:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to[0,1]
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\epsilon)
unclonable security
if for all QPT cloning adversaries
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
we have:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})+\varepsilon(\lambda).

If
|\mathcal{M}|=1
, we will simply write
\varepsilon
unclonable security.

4.3.1
Security for Search Games

For the special case of search games, we consider two definitions below.

Definition 10

(Unclonable Search Security)
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a cloning search game with
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security, we additionally say that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable search security
.

Remark 2
.

Note that even though the definitions above are valid for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
, to get meaningful security one needs to choose
\mathcal{D}
appropriately for the context. For instance, if the cloning game
\mathcal{G}
represents copy-protection for point functions, it is appropriate to pick
\mathcal{D}
in a balanced way so that the trivial success probability
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})
is bounded away from 1. As long as this is the case,
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security (for small
\varepsilon
) is non-trivial
17
17

17

We assume statistical correctness here.
in the sense that it is classically impossible and it uses the power of no-cloning. On the other hand, when
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})\approx 1
unclonable security becomes trivial and achieved by uninteresting constructions including classical games.

Definition 11

(Unclonable Indistinguishable Security)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m_{1}}
denote the distribution that outputs messages
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
with probability
1/2
each. We say that a search game
\mathcal{G}
has
\varepsilon
unclonable indistinguishable security
if it has
(\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m_{1}},\varepsilon)
unclonable search security
for any pair of messages
m_{0},m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}
.

In
Definitions

9
,
10
and
11
, if
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
is not required to be efficient, then we say that
\mathcal{G}
has
information theoretic
unclonable (search/indistinguishable) security.

4.4
Extended Definitions

Stateful Games.

Most cloning games can be captured by
Definition

2
. Yet, some cloning games are
stateful
in the sense that verification takes as additional input the random coins used in the challenge generation. With this in mind, we define a generalization of the cloning game below, highlighting the differences in
blue
. Throughout this section, we will assume that
\mathsf{GenC}
is a classical algorithm.
18
18

18

If
\mathsf{GenC}
is quantum, one can similarly define statefulness by having
\mathsf{GenC}
output some random coins that it sampled during its execution. One would need this because unlike classical algorithms, the randomness of a quantum algorithm can inherently result from collapsing measurements and hence cannot be modeled as an auxiliary random input string.

Definition 12

(Stateful Cloning Game)
.

A
stateful cloning game
consists of a tuple of efficient algorithms
\mathcal{G}=\left(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver}\right)
.

•

Key Generation:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
in unary. It outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
.

•

Token Generation:

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m
. It outputs a quantum token
\rho
.

•

Challenge Generation:

\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r_{\mathsf{GenC}}}})
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m
.

It outputs a classical challenge
\mathsf{ch}
.

•

Verification:

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},r_{\mathsf{GenC}}}})
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
, a message
m
, a challenge
\mathsf{ch}
, an answer
\mathsf{ans}
,
random coins
r_{\mathsf{GenC}}
used by
\mathsf{GenC}
when generating
\mathsf{ch}
. It outputs either
0
(reject) or
1
(accept).

When we talk about cloning games, we will always implicitly mean stateful cloning games. In fact, all of our results easily generalize to stateful cloning games. However, we will omit
r_{\mathsf{GenC}}
above and use the syntax in
Definition

2
when appropriate, for simplicity.

Security Against Correlated Distributions.

When we defined security in
Section

4.3
, we assumed that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
in the security experiment receive independently generated challenges. We will define security more broadly and refer to the aforementioned definition as
independent-challenge security
. For simplicity, we will assume that challenge generation is classical, i.e.,
\mathsf{GenC}
is a PPT algorithm, which is true for all the primitives considered in this work.

Definition 13

(Challenge Extension)
.

Let
\mathsf{GenC}
be a challenge generation algorithm that takes as input randomness from
\mathcal{R}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)}
. We say that
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
is an
extension
of
\mathsf{GenC}
if:

•

On input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m
, it outputs a pair of random strings
(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})\in\mathcal{R}^{2}
.

•

For any
(\mathsf{sk},m)
, if
(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
, then the marginal distributions of both
r_{\mathcal{B}}
and
r_{\mathcal{C}}
are equal to
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

We will also refer to
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
as an extension of a game
\mathcal{G}
whenever
\mathsf{GenC}
is the challenge generation algorithm for
\mathcal{G}
. We sometimes will omit and not specify the extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
, in which case it is either clear from the context or assumed to be
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}=\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{ind}}
by default (see
Definition

15
).

Extended Cloning Experiment.

Next, we give a more general definition of unclonable security, highlighting the differences to the corresponding definitions in
Section

4.3
in
blue
.

Definition 14

(Extended Cloning Experiment)
.

An
(extended) cloning experiment
, denoted by
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
, is a security game played between a referee
\mathsf{Ref}
and a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
. It is parameterized by a cloning game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
, a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over the message space
\mathcal{M}
,
and an extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
of
\mathsf{GenC}
. The experiment is described as follows:

•

Setup Phase:

–

All parties get a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples a message
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\rho
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

Splitting Phase:

–

\mathcal{A}
computes a bipartite state
\rho^{\prime}
over registers
B,C
.

–

\mathcal{A}
sends
\rho^{\prime}[B]
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\rho^{\prime}[C]
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Challenge Phase:

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples
(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
and then computes
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m;r_{\mathcal{B}})
,
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m;r_{\mathcal{C}})
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

–

\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
send back answers
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes bits
b_{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},r_{\mathcal{B}}}})
and
b_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1},r_{\mathcal{C}}}})
.

–

The outcome of the game is denoted by
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))
, which equals 1 if
b_{\mathcal{B}}=b_{\mathcal{C}}=1
, indicating that the adversary has won, and 0 otherwise, indicating that the adversary has lost.

Next, we discuss two important special cases for the extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
. In the first case,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
get the same challenge, whereas in the second case, they get independently generated challenges. Keep in mind that the second case is the default assumption when we do not mention extensions in
Section

4.3
.

Definition 15

(Identical/Independent-Challenge Cloning Experiment)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
and define the following extensions of
\mathsf{GenC}
, which takes randomness from the set
\mathcal{R}
:

•

\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{id}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
samples
r\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
and outputs
(r,r)
.

•

\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{ind}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
computes
r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
independently. It outputs
(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})
.

Then, we call the cloning experiments
\mathfrak{CE}^{\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{id}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathfrak{CE}^{\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{ind}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
an
identical-challenge cloning experiment
or an
independent-challenge cloning experiment
, respectively.

Trivial Success Probability.

We will slightly modify the definition of trivial attacks to account for the potential asymmetry introduced by a challenge extension.

Definition 16

((Extended) Trivial Cloning Attack)
.

We say that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
is a
\mathcal{B}
-
trivial cloning attack
against a cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
if
\mathcal{A}
upon receiving a token
\rho
, sends the product state
\ket{\bot}\!\!\bra{\bot}\otimes\rho
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
. In other words, only
\mathcal{C}
gets the token
\rho
. We denote by
\mathsf{TRIV}{{{}_{\mathcal{B}}}}(\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
the set of
\mathcal{B}
-
trivial cloning attacks against
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
. We similarly define
\mathsf{TRIV}{{{}_{\mathcal{C}}}}(\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
as the set of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{C}}}
-trivial attacks.

Finally, we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}):=\mathsf{TRIV}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})\cup\mathsf{TRIV}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})

as the set of trivial cloning attacks against
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
.

Definition 17

((Extended) Trivial Success Probability for Cloning Games)
.

We define the
\mathcal{B}
-
trivial success probability of a cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{B}}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}):=\sup_{\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)\in\mathsf{TRIV}{{{}_{\mathcal{B}}}}(\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})}\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right].

We similarly define
p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}})
as the
\mathcal{C}
-trivial success probability of
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
. Accordingly, we define the trivial success probability of
\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}})=

\displaystyle\max\left(p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{B}}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})}},p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})}}\right).

Remark 3
.

One may consider mixtures of
\mathcal{B}
-trivial and
\mathcal{C}
-trivial cloning attacks as trivial, but such attacks cannot do better than trivial cloning attacks by convexity.

Definition 18

((Extended) Unclonable Security)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a cloning game with extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
,
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over the message space
\mathcal{M}
, and
\varepsilon:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to[0,1]
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\epsilon,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}})
unclonable security
if for all QPT cloning adversaries
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}})+\varepsilon(\lambda).

We define
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon,\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
unclonable search security and
(\varepsilon,\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
unclonable indistinguishable security similarly. If
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}=\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{ind}}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}=\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{id}}
), then we say
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
independent-challenge (resp., identical-challenge) unclonable security. Likewise, information theoretic security is defined by removing the efficiency requirement from
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
as before.

A good number of cloning games of interest will have the following additional property.

Definition 19

(Evasiveness)
.

A cloning game
\mathcal{G}
is called
\mathcal{D}
-evasive
if
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})
is a negligible function in
\lambda
.

Note that the definition above is independent of the extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
for statistically correct games, i.e.
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
is negligible for any extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
. We keep the definition simple given that we only use it for statistically correct games.

Asymmetric Verification.

Another way to generalize the cloning games is to allow asymmetric verification for
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, which we will define and discuss in
Section

8
.

4.5
Examples

In this section, we demonstrate the comprehensiveness of cloning games by casting popular unclonable primitives as cloning games. We restrict our attention to primitives with symmetric verification, and those with asymmetric verification, such as secure software leasing or certified deletion, require a slightly more general syntax, which will be defined in
Section

8
.

4.5.1
Copy-Protection

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the class of functions of the form
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, parameterized implicitly by a security parameter
\lambda
, and let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over
\mathcal{F}
. A copy-protection scheme for
\mathcal{D}
is a pair of efficient algorithms
(\mathsf{CP},\mathsf{Eval})
:

•

\mathsf{CP}(1^{\lambda},d_{f})
takes as input description
d_{f}
of a function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
and outputs a copy-protected quantum program
\rho_{f}
.

•

\mathsf{Eval}(1^{\lambda},\rho_{f},x)
takes as input a quantum program
\rho_{f}
and an input
x\in\mathcal{X}
. It outputs a value
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

(\mathsf{CP},\mathsf{Eval})
defines a cloning game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{CP}}^{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
for any family of distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}=\left(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f}\right)_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
over
\mathcal{F}
as follows. Note that,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
defines a distribution on challenge inputs, therefore, it specifies
\mathsf{GenC}
.

•

The message space
\mathcal{M}
is the set of function descriptions
d_{f}
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
.

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
outputs
\mathsf{sk}=1^{\lambda}
, i.e. there is no secret key.

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
parses the input as
m=d_{f}
, then it computes
\rho_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{CP}(1^{\lambda},d_{f})
and outputs
\rho_{f}
.

•

\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
parses
m=d_{f}
and samples input
x\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f}
.
19
19

19

Here we make the natural assumption that correctness and security are defined with respect to the same distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{f}
. Intuitively, the scheme should protect against cloning the functionality of the honest evaluator.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})
parses
m=d_{f}
,
\mathsf{ch}=x
. It accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}=f(x)
.

Correctness:

We require that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{CP}}
has statistical correctness, to ensure that the copy-protected program is reusable. More specifically,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{CP}}}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})
runs
\mathsf{Eval}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})
.
20
20

20

Note that this captures the average-input correctness as opposed to per-input correctness.

Security:

We consider a game-based definition of copy-protection, first defined by
[
CMP20
,
BJL+21
]
. We say that
(\mathsf{CP},\mathsf{Eval})
is secure for a class of distributions
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{CP}}^{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security. For optimal security, we require
\varepsilon
to be negligible.

4.5.2
Unclonable Encryption

Below, we define unclonable encryption
[
BL20
]
as a cloning game. We focus our attention to one-time secret-key setting, in which case unclonable encryption is synonymous with cloning encryption games defined in
Definition

20
. It is known in the literature that construction in this simple setting can be generically lifted to achieve unclonable encryption with additional properties, such as public-key encryption
[
AK21
]
. We note, however, that unclonable encryption with such properties can still be expressed as a cloning game by modifying the syntax of a cloning encryption game. We state the correspondence below, which is easy to verify.

Definition 20

(Cloning Encryption Game)
.

A cloning search game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
is called a
cloning encryption game
if
\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
outputs
\mathsf{sk}
with probability 1 for all
(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

Fact 11

(Informal)
.

An unclonable encryption scheme for a message space
\mathcal{M}
exists with unclonable (unclonable indistinguishable) security if and only if a cloning encryption game
\mathcal{G}
for
\mathcal{M}
with unclonable (unclonable indistinguishable) security exists.

There are two types of security we will consider for unclonable encryption: (1)
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},\varepsilon)
unclonable security and (2)
\varepsilon
unclonable indistinguishable security. These security definitions together with
\delta
-correctness are on par with the original definitions of
[
BL20
]
.
21
21

21

Although (2) was defined in a slightly different way in
[
BL20
]
, the difference is inconsequential, and our version has been used in follow-up works such as
[
AKL+22
]
. We also mention that
[
BL20
]
considered perfect correctness.
Note that since
\mathsf{GenC}
is deterministic, it has a unique extension.

A particular case of interest is adapted
22
22

22

We omit a classical one-time-pad on the message
m
, which is irrelevant for the purposes of unclonability.
from the
conjugate encryption
of
[
BL20
]
and uses Wiesner (BB84) states:

Definition 21

(BB84 Cloning Game)
.

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
is a cloning encryption game with message space
\mathcal{M}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
, defined as follows:

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
outputs
\theta\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\theta,m)
takes as input
\theta,m\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
and outputs
\rho=\ket{m^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{m^{\theta}}
, where
\ket{m^{\theta}}=H^{\theta}\ket{m}

•

\mathsf{GenC}
and
\mathsf{Ver}
are defined as part of a cloning encrpytion game.

Lemma 12

(Security of BB84 Cloning Game
[
BL20
]
)
.

The game
\mathcal{G}_{BB84}
above has
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},|\mathcal{M}|^{-\delta})
unclonable security for some constant
\delta>0
.

4.5.3
Single Decryptor Encryption

We define single-decryptor encryption as a tuple of efficient algorithms
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
, adapted from the definition of (secret-key) single-decryptor encryption (with honestly generated keys) in
[
GZ20
]
:

1.

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
takes as input a security parameter and outputs a classical secret key
sk
.

2.

\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(sk)
takes as input a classical secret key and it outputs a quantum decryption key
\rho_{dk}
.

3.

\mathsf{Enc}(sk,x)
takes as input a secret key and a classical message. It outputs a classical ciphertext
ct
.

4.

\mathsf{Dec}(\rho,ct)
takes as input a quantum decryption key and a classical ciphertext. It outputs a classical message
x^{\prime}
.

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
defines a stateful cloning game
\mathcal{G}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}}_{\mathsf{SDE}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
, parameterized by a distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
, where
\mathcal{X}
is the set of classical messages encrypted by this scheme, as follows:

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
runs
sk\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and outputs
\mathsf{sk}=sk
.

•

There is no message, i.e.
m=\bot
.

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
computes
\rho_{dk}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(sk)
and outputs
\rho_{dk}
.

•

\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m;r_{\mathsf{GenC}})
samples
x\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{X}
using random coins
r_{\mathsf{GenC}}
. It outputs
c\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(sk,x)
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans},r_{\mathsf{GenC}})
computes
x
as above using
r_{\mathsf{GenC}}
. Then it accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}=x
.

Correctness.

We say that
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
has correctness if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{SDE}}^{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}}
has perfect correctness for any distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
. More specifically,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{SDE}}}(\rho_{dk},\mathsf{ch})
runs
\mathsf{Dec}(\rho_{dk},\mathsf{ch})
.

Security.

We say that
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
has
\varepsilon
unclonable security if
\mathcal{G}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{X}}}_{\mathsf{SDE}}
has
\varepsilon
unclonable security
23
23

23

We omit the message distribution due to the lack of message.
.

In other words, the ability to decrypt a random classical message is the unclonable property of the quantum decryption key. For optimal security, we require that
\varepsilon
is negligible.

4.5.4
Quantum Money

Next, we give examples of cloning games with quantum verification. We focus on quantum money, first introduced by Wiesner
[
Wie83
]
. We consider a public-key variant of quantum money considered by
[
AC12
,
Zha17
]
. We note that the description below can be suitably adapted to case private-key quantum money as a cloning game. A public-key quantum money scheme is a tuple of efficient algorithms
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Mint},\mathsf{VerToken})
:

•

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
takes as input a security parameter and outputs a public-secret key pair
(pk,sk)
.

•

\mathsf{Mint}(sk)
takes as input a secret key and outputs a classical serial number
s
and a quantum banknote
\rho_{s}
.

•

\mathsf{VerToken}(pk,s,\rho)
takes as input a public key, a serial number, and a quantum state. It outputs 0 (reject) or 1 (accept).

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{Mint},\mathsf{VerToken})
defines a cloning game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{QM}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
as follows:

•

We set
m=\bot
, i.e. there is no message.

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
runs
(pk,sk)\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and outputs
\mathsf{sk}=(pk,sk)

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(pk,sk)
, runs
(s,\rho_{s})\leftarrow\mathsf{Mint}(sk)
, and outputs
\rho\otimes\ket{s}\!\!\bra{s}\otimes\ket{pk}\!\!\bra{pk}
.

•

\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
outputs
\mathsf{ch}=\bot
, i.e. no challenge.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\sigma_{\mathsf{ans}})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(pk,sk)
and outputs
b\leftarrow\mathsf{VerToken}(pk,\sigma_{\mathsf{ans}})

Correctness.

We say that the quantum money scheme has correctness if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{QM}}
has statistical correctness. Note that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{QM}}}
can simply output the quantum banknote it receives to satisfy
Definition

3
(correctness). Furthermore, it can be assumed without loss of generality that the optimal
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{QM}}}
acts as identity (i.e. outputs
\rho
as is) since there is no challenge. Therefore, this fully captures the usual definition of correctness for quantum money schemes.

Security.

We say that the quantum money scheme is secure if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{QM}}
has
\varepsilon

unclonable security. For optimal security, we require that
\varepsilon
is negligible. Note that unclonable security as we defined only gives 1-to-2 unclonability, but it can be generalized to
k
-to-
k+1
unclonability. Alternatively, one can define a quantum-money mini scheme in our framework, which is necessary and sufficient for constructing public-key quantum money
[
AC12
]
.

5
Constructive Post-Quantum Reductions: The Non-Local Setting

We present a new toolkit to understand the relationship between different cloning games. We first define a notion of non-local classical reductions. Roughly speaking, a classical non-local reduction transforms a non-local classical algorithm
24
24

24

Refer to
Section

3
for the definition of a non-local algorithm.
, solving a problem
P
to a non-local classical algorithm solving a problem
Q
. The reason we consider non-local classical reductions is that it turns out to be simpler to come up with non-local reductions in the classical setting. We then show how to generically upgrade some classes of classical non-local reductions to quantum non-local reductions, the analogous notion for quantum non-local algorithms. The resulting quantum non-local reductions are useful in analyzing the relationship between different cloning games.

5.1
Definitions

In this section, we borrow definitions from Section 3 of
[
BBK22
]
and adapt them to the non-local setting
25
25

25

For simplicity, we omit explicit purification of quantum solvers (see Definition 3.5), as we will make nearly black-box use of the results of
[
BBK22
]
.
.

Extension.

Similar to
Definition

13
, we define the notion of an extension over random coins in this context.

Definition 22

(
d
-extension)
.

We say that a distribution
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
over
\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}
is a
d
-extension
if for
(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, the marginal distributions of both
r_{\mathcal{B}}
and
r_{\mathcal{C}}
are
\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}}
.

5.1.1
Classical Assumptions

The first two definitions below are commonly used in the cryptography literature. Note that we use
\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver}
to denote the algorithms in order to point out the connection between a non-interactive assumption and the challenge phase of a cloning game (see
Definitions

2
and
6
).

Definition 23

(Non-Interactive Assumption)
.

A non-interactive assumption
\mathfrak{A}=(d,n,m,\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
is associated with polynomials
d(\lambda),n(\lambda),m(\lambda)
and a tuple
P=(\mathsf{GenC},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver},\allowbreak c)
with the following syntax. Here,
\mathsf{GenC}
and
\mathsf{Ver}
are classical algorithms, and
c:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
is the assumption’s threshold.

•

Challenge generator,
\mathsf{GenC}(1^{\lambda};r)
: on input security parameter
\lambda
and random coins
r\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, outputs a challenge
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

•

Verifier,
\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},r,y)
: on input security parameter
\lambda
, random coins
r\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, and answer
y\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, outputs
1
(accept) or
0
(reject).

We will sometimes use
P
and
\mathfrak{A}
interchangeably, with the understanding that the polynomials
d,n,m
are implicit.
We say that
P
is
evasive
if
c
is a negligible function.

Another important property is called
verifiably polynomial image
, and informally it requires that it is possible to verify if a solution lies in a polynomial-size superset of valid solutions without the random coins
r
of
\mathsf{GenC}
.

Definition 24

(Verifiably Polynomial Image)
.

A non-interactive assumption
\mathfrak{A}=(d,n,m,\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
has a
verifiably polynomial image
if there exists a polynomial
k(\lambda)
and an efficient verifier
K
such that for every
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
, the set
Y_{x}:=|\{y\ :\ K(1^{\lambda},x,y)=1\}|
has size at most
k
and for any valid challenge
x=G(1^{\lambda};r)
and answer
\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},r,y)=1
, it holds that
y\in Y_{x}
.

Remark 4
.

At a high level, the above definition states that a non-interactive assumption with verifiably polynomial range has a public verification algorithm
K
that accepts all the solutions also accepted by
\mathsf{Ver}
(and possibly, more) such that for any
x
, the set of strings accepted by
K
should be of polynomial size.

5.1.2
Solvers

Local Solvers.

A local solver for a non-interactive assumption
P
, which we call a
P
-solver
, is a pair
\mathcal{A}=(\widetilde{alice},{\sf state}_{0})
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is an algorithm which takes as input a challenge
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and outputs an answer
y\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{m}
, whereas
{\sf state}_{0}
is an initial state. We define the value and advantage of a local solver below:

Definition 25

(Value and Advantage of a Local Solver (Definition 3.3 in
[
BBK22
]
))
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(d,n,m,\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
be a non-interactive assumption, with
P=(\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
, and let
\mathcal{A}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},{\sf state}_{0})
be a
P
-solver. We define the (one-shot) value and advantage of
\mathcal{A}
, parameterized by the security parameter
\lambda
, as

\displaystyle\mathsf{val}_{P}[\mathcal{A}]=\Pr\left[\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},r,y)=1\;\Bigg|\;\begin{subarray}{c}r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\\
x=\mathsf{GenC}(1^{\lambda};r)\\
y\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},x,{\sf state}_{0})\end{subarray}\right],\quad\mathsf{adv}_{P}[\mathcal{A}]=\left|\mathsf{val}_{P}[\mathcal{A}]-c\right|.

Non-Local Solvers.

Below, we adapt
Definition

25
to the non-local setting. A non-local adversary consists of two (possibly inefficient) algorithms
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
, where both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are given (possibly correlated) instances from a non-interactive assumption. Each adversary is expected to solve the instance they get. Note that both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are not allowed to communicate with each other. In the end, the adversary wins if both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
win. In general, for algorithms
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
acting on separate registers, we call the algorithm
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
a non-local algorithm
26
26

26

A non-local algorithm can be implemented by two spatially separated and non-communicating parties, hence the name.
. We consider the non-uniform setting, where both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
could share some auxiliary information (either a string or a quantum state, depending on whether the adversary is classical or quantum) in the very beginning.

In order to define reductions, we need to define stateful solvers. Suppose in a reduction, we use the solver for a problem
P
to design a solver for problem
Q
. Suppose the
Q
solver runs the
P
solver multiple times. In the classical setting, the
P
solver could be stateless, whereas in the quantum setting the
P
solver is inherently stateful and thus, we need to define stateful solvers appropriately below. Formally, we adapt Definition 3.4 from
[
BBK22
]
to the non-local setting.

Definition 26

(Non-Local Stateful Solvers: Syntax)
.

Let
P
be a non-interactive assumption with
d
-bit random coins.

Let
\ell=\ell(\lambda),\ell_{\mathcal{B}}=\ell_{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda),\ell_{\mathcal{C}}=\ell_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)
be functions such that
\ell_{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda)+\ell_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda)=\ell(\lambda)

and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
be a
d
-extension.
An
(\ell,\ell_{\mathcal{B}},\ell_{\mathcal{C}})
-stateful non-local
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-solver
{\cal A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},{\sf state}_{0}=\{{\sf state}_{0,\lambda}\}_{\lambda})
is defined as follows.

•

{\sf state}_{0}=\{{\sf state}_{0,\lambda}\}_{\lambda}
is a sequence of bipartite
\ell
-qubit states (with the partitions being
\ell_{\mathcal{B}}
-qubit register
{\bf B}
and
\ell_{\mathcal{C}}
-qubit register
{\bf C}
).

•

{\cal A}
is a quantum algorithm that takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
, a step
1^{t}
, a pair of challenges
x_{\mathcal{B}},x_{\mathcal{C}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and
{\sf state}=({\sf state}_{\mathcal{B}},{\sf state}_{\mathcal{C}})
, which is an
\ell
-qubit bipartite state.
It runs
(\mathcal{B}(1^{\lambda},x_{\mathcal{B}},\cdot)\otimes\mathcal{C}(1^{\lambda},x_{\mathcal{C}},\cdot))({\sf state})
, with
\mathcal{B}
getting as input register
{\bf B}
and
\mathcal{C}
getting as input
{\bf C}
, to obtain
(y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}^{\prime})
, where
y_{\mathcal{B}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
is
\mathcal{B}
’s output,
y_{\mathcal{C}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
is
\mathcal{C}
’s output and
{\sf state}^{\prime}=({\sf state}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{B}},{\sf state}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{C}})
is a bipartite
\ell
-qubit state on
\ell_{\mathcal{B}}
-qubit register
{\bf B}
and
\ell_{\mathcal{C}}
-qubit register
{\bf C}
with
{\bf B}
(resp.,
{\bf C}
) being the output of
\mathcal{B}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}
).
{\cal A}
outputs
(y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}^{\prime})
.

If the states
{\sf state}_{i,\lambda}
are classical strings and
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
are classical algorithms, then
\mathcal{A}
is called a classical non-local stateful solver, otherwise it is called a quantum non-local stateful solver. If
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}}
, then
\mathcal{A}
is called an
independent-challenge

P
-solver.

Remark 5
.

Even though the definition above of a non-local
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-solver does not depend on
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, it will be used to define the value and advantage of the solver below.

Examples of Non-Local Solvers:

For instance, if
P
is inverting a one-way function
f
, then
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
respectively get
x_{\mathcal{B}}=f(z_{\mathcal{B}})
and
x_{\mathcal{C}}=f(z_{\mathcal{C}})
as challenges, where
(z_{\mathcal{B}},z_{\mathcal{C}})
is pair of uniform inputs arbitrarily correlated depending on
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
. In order to pass verification,
\mathcal{B}
needs to output
y_{\mathcal{B}}=z_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathcal{C}
needs to output
y_{\mathcal{C}}=z_{\mathcal{C}}
. Another example is when
P
is distinguishing the output of a PRG
G
from a random string. In this case,
x_{\mathcal{B}}
and
x_{\mathcal{C}}
are each either a uniform output of
G
or a uniformly random string. In order to pass verification,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
need to correctly guess which one. The correct answer for
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, may be arbitrarily correlated depending on
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
. Furthermore,
x_{\mathcal{B}},x_{\mathcal{C}}
could be correlated still conditioned on the answers.

We formalize the interaction between a non-local solver and a non-local algorithm below. Formally, we adapt Definition 3.6 from
[
BBK22
]
.

Definition 27

(Non-Local Solver Interaction)
.

Let
P=(\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
be a non-interactive assumption. For any stateful
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-solver
{\cal A}=(\mathcal{B}_{{\cal A}},\mathcal{C}_{{\cal A}},{\sf state}_{0})
, and
{\cal S}=(\mathcal{B}_{\cal S},\mathcal{C}_{\cal S})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{\cal S}
and non-local algorithm
\mathcal{C}_{\cal S}
are a pair of algorithms, with input
z=(z_{\mathcal{B}},z_{\mathcal{C}})\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, we consider the process
{\cal S}^{{\cal A}}_{z}(1^{\lambda})
of the algorithm interacting with the solver. We define this process below.

•

{\cal S}
is invoked on the input
(1^{\lambda},z_{\mathcal{B}},z_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}_{0})
, where
\mathcal{B}_{\cal S}
receives as input
z_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\cal S}
receives as input
z_{\mathcal{C}}
. Moreover,
{\sf state}_{0}
is defined on two registers
{\bf B}
and
{\bf C}
, with
{\mathcal{B}}_{\cal S}
receiving the register
{\bf B}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\cal S}
receiving the register
{\bf C}
. Initialize
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
to contain
z_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
to contain
z_{\mathcal{C}}
. At every step
i\geq 1
:

1.

{\mathcal{B}}_{\cal S}(1^{\lambda},\tau_{\mathcal{B}})
makes a query
x_{\mathcal{B}}^{(i)}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\cal S}(1^{\lambda},\tau_{\mathcal{B}})
makes a query
x_{\mathcal{C}}^{(i)}
,

2.

Run
(y_{\mathcal{B}}^{(i)},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{(i)},{\sf state}_{i})\leftarrow(\mathcal{B}_{{\cal A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{B}}^{(i)},\cdot)\otimes\mathcal{C}_{{\cal A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{C}}^{(i)},\cdot))({\sf state}_{i-1})
, where
y_{\mathcal{B}}^{(i)}
is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}}
’s output,
y_{\mathcal{C}}^{(i)}
is
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
’s output and
{\sf state}_{i}
is the joint output of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

3.

Add
(x_{\mathcal{B}}^{(i)},y_{\mathcal{B}}^{(i)})
to
\tau_{\mathcal{B}}
and
(x_{\mathcal{C}}^{(i)},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{(i)})
to
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
.

•

At the end of the interaction,
{\cal S}
may produce the output
(w_{\mathcal{B}},w_{\mathcal{C}})
, where
w_{\mathcal{B}}
is output by
{\mathcal{B}}_{\cal S}
and
w_{\mathcal{C}}
is output by
{\mathcal{C}}_{\cal S}
.

We will sometimes refer to
S
as a solver-aided non-local algorithm.

Non-Local Solver Value, Advantage, and Persistence.

For stateful solvers, we explicitly define their value after interacting with the assumption many times.

Definition 28

(Non-local Stateful Value and Advantage)
.

Let
P=(\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
be a non-interactive assumption and let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{A}},{\sf state}_{0})
be a stateful
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-non-local solver. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a solver-aided non-local algorithm with input
z=(z_{\mathcal{B}},z_{\mathcal{C}})
. Let
{\sf state}_{i}
be defined as in
Definition

27
for the interaction
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{z}(1^{\lambda})
. Then, we define the (many-shot) value of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{z}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{A}}\right]:=\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},y_{\mathcal{B}};r_{\mathcal{B}})=1\\
\land\\
\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},y_{\mathcal{C}};r_{\mathcal{C}})=1\end{subarray}\ {\Bigg|}\ \begin{subarray}{c}(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}\\
x_{\mathcal{B}}=\mathsf{GenC}(1^{\lambda};r_{\mathcal{B}})\\
x_{\mathcal{C}}=\mathsf{GenC}(1^{\lambda};r_{\mathcal{C}})\\
(y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}_{{i+1}})\leftarrow(\mathcal{B}_{{\cal A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i+1},x_{\mathcal{B}},\cdot)\otimes\mathcal{C}_{{\cal A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i+1},x_{\mathcal{C}},\cdot))({\sf state}_{i})\end{subarray}\right],

and for any threshold
c^{*}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
, we define the (many-shot) advantage of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{z}
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c^{*})}\left[i,{\mathcal{A}}\right]:=\left|\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{A}}\right]-c^{*}(\lambda)\right|

We define the one-shot value of the solver interaction
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{A}}_{z}
as
\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]:=\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[0,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{A}}\right]
and the one-shot advantage of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to threshold
c^{*}
as
\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c^{*})}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]:=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c^{*})}\left[0,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{A}}\right]
. Note that neither value depends on
(\mathcal{S},z)
.

Remark 6

(Non-Local vs. Local Threshold)
.

Above, the non-local threshold
c^{*}
, which is a parameter of the advantage
\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c^{*})}
of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
, can depend on the local threshold
c
as well as the
d
-extension
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
.

Definition 29

(Non-Local Persistence)
.

Let
P
be a non-interactive assumption. A distribution
\mathbb{B}
on
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-non-local solvers
\left\{\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}=(\mathcal{B}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{A}},{\sf state}_{0}^{\alpha})\right\}_{\alpha}
is called
(p,\eta)
-persistent if for any solver-aided non-local algorithm
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{S}})
with input
z=(z_{\mathcal{B}},z_{\mathcal{C}})
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\sup_{i}\left|\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}}\right]-p\right|\leq\eta\right]\geq 1-\eta,

where the probability is taken over the randomness of
\alpha
and the solver interaction
\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}}
.

5.1.3
Reductions

A reduction is a transformation that converts a solver for one problem to a solver for another (possibly different) problem. More precisely, suppose
P
and
Q
be two assumptions. A reduction leverages the solver for
P
to design a solver for
Q
. We consider the setting when the solver for
Q
uses the solver for
P
as a black-box. As in
[
BBK22
]
, we consider reductions where the solver for
Q
runs the solver for
P
multiple times. We define analogous notions of one-shot advantage and persistent advantage below.

We first define the traditional notion of reduction before defining non-local reductions.

Definition 30

(Reduction; Definition 3.12 in
[
BBK22
]
)
.

A classical (resp., quantum) reduction from solving a non-interactive assumption
Q
to solving a non-interactive assumption
P
is an efficient classical (resp., quantum) uniform algorithm
{\cal R}
with the following guarantee.

For any classical (resp., quantum)
P
-solver
{\cal A}_{P}=(A_{P},{\sf state}_{0})
with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon
and runtime
T
, let
{\sf state}^{\prime}_{0}=({\sf state}_{0},A_{P},1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{T})
. Then,
{\cal A}_{Q}=({\cal R},{\sf state}^{\prime}_{0})
is a classical (resp., quantum)
Q
-solver with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathsf{poly}(\varepsilon,\allowbreak T^{-1},\allowbreak\lambda^{-1})
and runtime
\mathsf{poly}(T,\varepsilon^{-1},\allowbreak\lambda)
. An
inefficient
reduction is defined the same way without the runtime requirement and the dependency on
T
.

Non-Local Reductions.

We now generalize the above definition to the non-local setting.

Definition 31

(Non-Local Reduction)
.

A non-local classical (resp., quantum)
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P}^{*},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q}^{*})
-reduction from solving a non-interactive assumption
Q
to solving a non-interactive assumption
P
is an efficient classical (resp., quantum) uniform non-local algorithm
{\cal R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2})
with the following guarantee.

Given any non-local classical (resp., quantum)
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-solver
{\cal A}_{P}=(\mathcal{B}_{P},\mathcal{C}_{P},{\sf state}_{0})
with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c_{P}^{*})}\left[\mathcal{A}_{P}\right]
and runtime
T
, let
{\sf state}^{\prime}_{0}=({\sf state}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{P},\mathcal{C}_{P},1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{T})
.
Then,
{\cal A}_{Q}=({\cal R}_{1},{\cal R}_{2},{\sf state}^{\prime}_{0})
is a
(Q,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q})
-solver with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathsf{adv}_{Q}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c_{Q}^{*})}\left[\mathcal{A}_{Q}\right]=\mathsf{poly}(\varepsilon,T^{-1},\lambda^{-1})
and runtime
\mathsf{poly}(T,\varepsilon^{-1},\lambda)
. Here, it is understood that description of
\mathcal{B}_{P}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}_{P}
) is included as part of
{\cal R}_{1}
’s (resp.,
{\cal R}_{2}
’s) register. An
inefficient
non-local reduction is defined the same way without the runtime requirement and the dependency of
T
.

Definition 32

(Non-Local Black-Box Reduction)
.

A non-local black-box
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P}^{*},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q}^{*})
-reduction
\mathcal{R}=(\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2})
from solving
Q=(\mathsf{GenC}_{Q},\mathsf{Ver}_{Q},c_{Q})
to solving
P=(\mathsf{GenC}_{P},\mathsf{Ver}_{P},c_{P})
is a non-local
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P}^{*},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q}^{*})
-reduction such that
\mathcal{R}_{1}
(resp.,
{\cal R}_{2}
) interacts with
\mathcal{B}_{P}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}_{P}
) as defined in
Definition

27
.

We further say that
{\cal R}
is
non-adaptive
if
\mathcal{R}_{1}
(resp.,
{\cal R}_{2}
) produces its queries to
\mathcal{B}_{P}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}_{P}
) independent of the answers to its previous queries (or equivalently all at once).

5.2
Main Theorem

In our main theorem, we show how to relate the unclonable security of two cloning games
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
which satisfy a similarity condition in the setup phase as well as some additional properties. Before we state the formal theorem, we first describe how the notions of non-interactive assumption and unclonable security are connected by introducing relevant notation.

Assumption Induced by a Cloning Experiment.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a stateful
27
27

27

Recall that stateful cloning games (
Definition

12
) generalize cloning games, so that this definition applies the same to cloning games that are not stateful.
cloning game with message space
\mathcal{M}
. For every
m\in\mathcal{M}
, message distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
, and secret key
\mathsf{sk}
in the support of
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
, we consider the induced non-interactive assumption
\mathfrak{A}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}}=(d,n,\ell,\mathsf{GenC}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},c)
. Here
d
is the length of the random coins used by
\mathsf{GenC}
,
n
is the length of the challenges output by
\mathsf{GenC}
, and
\ell
is the length of the answers received by
\mathsf{GenC}
. In addition,
\mathsf{GenC}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}}(1^{\lambda};r):=\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m;r)
and
\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}}(1^{\lambda},r,\mathsf{ans}):=\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans},r)
.
28
28

28

Note that this is well-defined since
\mathsf{ch}
can be computed deterministically given
\mathsf{sk},m,r
.
Finally, we will set
c:=p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}})
for the message distribution
\mathcal{M}
considered for unclonable security. As before, we write
P^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}}=(\mathsf{GenC}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},c)
to denote the assumption when the parameters
d,n,\ell
are implicit.

Theorem 13

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
be cloning games with the same message space
\mathcal{M}
. Let
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
be a message distribution, and
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
be an extension of
\mathsf{GenC}
. Suppose that the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

\mathsf{Setup}=\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{GenT}=\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}
.

(2)

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
-evasive.

(3)

Either
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
-evasive or
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}_{\mathsf{ind}}
.

For
\mathsf{sk}
in the support of
\mathsf{Setup}
and message
m\in\mathcal{M}
, consider the induced non-interactive assumptions

\displaystyle P=P^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}}=(\mathsf{GenC}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}))

and

\displaystyle Q=Q^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}}=(\mathsf{GenC}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},\mathsf{Ver}^{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\mathsf{sk},m}},p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}})).

Suppose further that the following conditions are satisfied:

(a)

For all
(\mathsf{sk},m)
,
P
has a verifiably polynomial image.

(b)

For all
(\mathsf{sk},m)
, there exists a classical non-adaptive black-box reduction
\mathcal{R}
from solving non-interactive assumption
Q
to solving non-interactive assumption
P
. Furthermore, the description of
\mathcal{R}
does not depend on
(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

(c)

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
independent-challenge unclonable security.

Then,
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
unclonable security.

The proof of the main theorem consists of 3 technical steps: (1) going from the classical reduction given in
item

(b)
to a classical non-local reduction, (2) going from non-local
(Q,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q})
-solver to independent-challenge
Q
-solver, and (3) lifting classical reduction to quantum. We will formally discuss each step in
Section

5.3
. We include the final proof below to aid the reader in reading
Section

5.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

13
.

Define
c_{P}=p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
and
c_{Q}=p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}})
. By condition
(2)
,
Q
is evasive, i.e.
c_{Q}
is negligible. By conditions
(2)
,
(b)
and
Lemma

16
, there exists a classical non-adaptive black-box non-local
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{O},c_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q})
-reduction from solving
Q
to solving
P
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q}
denote uniform extensions.

By
Theorem

22
, there exists a quantum non-local
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{O},c_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q})
-reduction
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
from solving
Q
to solving
P
. Combining this with
Corollary

3
if
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
-evasive, and trivially if
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}_{\mathsf{ind}}
,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is also a quantum non-local
(\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}},c_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q})
-reduction from solving
Q
to solving
P
.

Now, let
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
be a QPT adversary which breaks
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\varepsilon)
unclonable security of
\mathcal{G}
for a non-negligible function
\varepsilon
. Let
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{sk},m}
be the one-shot value of
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
in assumption
P
defined above with respect to
c_{P}
, so that
\left|\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[\varepsilon_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{P}\right]\right|>\varepsilon
. Note that since the description of
\mathcal{R}
does not depend on
(\mathsf{sk},m)
, neither does the description of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
be the induced QPT adversary obtained by giving the solver
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{A}(\rho_{\mathsf{sk},m}))
as input to
\mathcal{R}
, where
\rho_{\mathsf{sk},m}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
is the token received by
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathcal{G}
. That is,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is defined in terms of
\mathcal{A}
and how the state
\mathcal{A}(\rho_{\mathsf{sk},m})
is modified by the reduction
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, whereas
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
is the non-local algorithm output by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. Note that this is well-defined since the description of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
does not depend on
(\mathsf{sk},m)
. Define
\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}
as the one-shot value of
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

By the guarantee of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, we have
\left|\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{Q}\right|\geq q(\left|\varepsilon_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{P}\right|)
for all
(\mathsf{sk},m)
and some polynomial
q
. Without loss of generality, we can take
q(x)=x^{\beta}
for some constant
\beta>1
, so that
q
is a monotone, convex function. Taking the expectation and using Jensen’s Inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle q(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle<q\left(\left|\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[\varepsilon_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{P}\right]\right|\right)\leq q\left(\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[\left|\varepsilon_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{P}\right|\right]\right)\leq\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[q\left(\left|\varepsilon_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{P}\right|\right)\right]\leq\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[\left|\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{Q}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}+c_{Q}\right]\leq 2c_{Q}+\left|\E_{\mathsf{sk},m}\left[\varepsilon^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}-c_{Q}\right]\right|,

hence
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
breaks the
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},q(\varepsilon)-2c_{Q})
unclonable security of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, which suffices for the proof since
c_{Q}
is negligible and
\varepsilon
is non-negligible.

∎

Alternate Versions of the Main Theorem.

We give two more versions of the main theorem, which could be useful for applications. The first one (
Theorem

14
) is for information theoretic applications, including our single-decryptor encryption construction in
Section

5.4.1
. The second one (
Theorem

15
) concerns the case when
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
do not have identical setup phases, yet they are equivalent modulo a local quantum transformation applied by
\mathcal{A}
, hence relaxing condition
(1)
of
Theorem

13
.

Theorem 14

(Main Theorem - Inefficient Version)
.

Let
P,Q,\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
be defined as in
Theorem

13
. Suppose that the conditions in
items

(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
and
item

(b)
are satisfied. If
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has information theoretic
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
independent-challenge unclonable security, then
\mathcal{G}
has information theoretic
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
unclonable security.

Proof.

The proof follows the same as that of
Theorem

13
. The only difference is that since
P
has no verifiably polynomial image, the non-local reduction is inefficient as per
Theorem

22
.
∎

Theorem 15

(Main Theorem - General Version)
.

Theorem

13
holds true if condition
(1)
is replaced with the following: There exists a quantum channel
\Gamma
such that,

\mathsf{TD}\left(\rho_{\mathsf{sk},m},\Gamma\left(\rho^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}\right)\right)\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

for all key-message pairs
(\mathsf{sk},m)
, where
\rho_{\mathsf{sk},m}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
and
\rho^{\prime}_{\mathsf{sk},m}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

Proof (sketch).

Follows by modifying the proof of
Theorem

13
so that the non-local reduction first applies
\Gamma
.
∎

We note that the same generalization can be applied to
Theorem

14
in the information theoretic setting.

5.3
Proof of the Main Theorem.

In this section, we give the technical details of the 3 steps of the proof of the main theorem.

Step 1: Lifting Classical Local Reduction to Non-Local Reduction.

In
Section

5.3.1
, we show that any classical reduction from solving
Q
to solving
P
implies a classical non-local reduction, where the assumption
Q
is assigned the independent-challenge distribution.

Step 2: Converting Non-Local Solver to Independent-Challenge Solver.

In
Section

5.3.2
, we show that any non-local solver with negligible non-local threshold is an independent-challenge solver. This step is needed because the third step below requires independent-challenge mode to lift a classical non-local reduction to quantum.

Step 3: Lifting Classical Non-Local Reductions to Quantum Reductions.

In
Sections

5.3.3
,
5.3.4
and
5.3.5
, we adapt the work of
[
BBK22
]
, which shows how to lift local non-adaptive black-box reductions to quantum, to the non-local setting. The main technical contribution occurs in
Section

5.3.3
, where we do non black-box analysis of the state-repair procedure of
[
CMSZ21
]
to show that it can be used in the non-local setting.

5.3.1
Upgrading Classical Local Reductions to Classical Non-Local Reductions

Most classical reductions known are for local assumptions, and we will show below how to obtain a non-local reduction from a local reduction. The non-local reduction simply runs the local reduction in each register.

Lemma 16
.

Suppose there exists a classical reduction
\mathcal{R}
from solving non-interactive evasive assumption
Q=(\mathsf{GenC}_{Q},\mathsf{Ver}_{Q},c_{Q})
to solving non-interactive assumption
P=(\mathsf{GenC}_{P},\mathsf{Ver}_{P},c_{P})
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q}=\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}^{d_{Q}}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{d_{Q}}}
be the uniform extension. Then, there exists a classical non-local
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q})
-reduction
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2})
from solving
Q
to solving
P
for any
d_{P}
-extension
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is non-adaptive (resp., black-box) if
\mathcal{R}
is non-adaptive (resp., black-box).

Proof.

The non-local reduction locally runs the classical reduction, i.e.
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{2}=\mathcal{R}
. Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
be a
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P})
-solver with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P})}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
be the non-local adversary output by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. Assume that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
are both stateless. Since
\varepsilon+c_{P}
is the probability of
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
simultaneously passing verification, it follows that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are both
P
-solvers with advantage
\varepsilon
. Thus, by assumption
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are both
Q
-solvers with advantage
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathsf{poly}(\varepsilon,T^{-1},\lambda)
, where
T
is the runtime of
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is an independent-challenge non-local
Q
-solver, it follows that the advantage of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is at least
(\varepsilon^{\prime}+c_{Q})^{2}-c_{Q}\geq(\varepsilon^{\prime})^{2}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
as desired.

The argument can be generalized to stateful non-local solvers by convexity.
∎

5.3.2
From Non-Local Solvers to Independent-Challenge Solvers

For the second step of the proof of the main theorem, we will show that any non-local solver is an independent-challenge solver, up to polynomial loss.

Theorem 17
.

Let
P
be a non-interactive assumption and let
c:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
be a negligible non-local threshold. Then, any non-local quantum
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-solver
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},{\sf state}_{0})
with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c)}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
is a quantum independent-challenge
P
-solver with advantage
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{ind}},c)}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]=\mathsf{poly}(\varepsilon)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{ind}}
is the uniform extension.

Proof.

Since
c
is negligible, it suffices to show
\mathsf{val}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{ind}},c)}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]=\mathsf{poly}\left(\mathsf{val}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c)}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]\right)
. Since the success of a non-local solver can be implemented as a product measurement, this follows from
Lemma

18
below, with the polynomial above given by
q(x)=x^{3}/216.

∎

Lemma 18
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a finite set of random coins,
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
be a distribution over
\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}
with marginals
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D})
, and
\rho
be a mixed bipartite state. Define the following quantities:

\displaystyle p_{cor}=\E_{(r,r^{\prime})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\left(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}}\right)\rho\right],\quad p_{ind}=\E_{r,r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\left(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}}\right)\rho\right],

where for each
r\in\mathcal{R}
, we have
0\leq B_{r}\leq I
and
0\leq C_{r}\leq I
. Then,

\displaystyle p_{id}\leq 6\sqrt[3]{p_{ind}}

Proof.

We can interpret
B_{r}
and
C_{r}
as POVM elements. Thus, by Naimark Dilation theorem, without loss of generality we can assume that
B_{r}
and
C_{r}
are projections. We can also assume that
\rho=\ket{\psi}\!\!\bra{\psi}
is a pure state, and the mixed state case follows by convexity, since
f(x)=6\sqrt[3]{x}
is a concave function.

Define PSD operators

\displaystyle P_{B}:=\E_{r\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}B_{r},\quad P_{C}:=\E_{r\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}C_{r},

and let

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\phi_{i}}\ket{\sigma_{j}}

be the spectral decomposition of
\ket{\psi}
with respect to
P_{B}\otimes P_{C}
, where
\ket{\phi_{i}}
is an eigenvector of
P_{B}
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
and
\ket{\sigma_{j}}
is an eigenvector of
P_{C}
with eigenvalue
\gamma_{j}
. Set
\eta=\sqrt[3]{p_{ind}}
and define subnormalized states

\displaystyle\ket{\psi_{B}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\;:\;\lambda_{i}\leq\eta\\
j\;:\;\gamma_{j}>\eta\end{subarray}}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\phi_{i}}\ket{\sigma_{j}},\quad\ket{\psi_{B}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\\
j\;:\;\gamma_{j}\leq\eta\end{subarray}}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\phi_{i}}\ket{\sigma_{j}},\quad\ket{\psi_{BC}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\;:\;\lambda_{i}>\eta\\
j\;:\;\gamma_{j}>\eta\end{subarray}}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\phi_{i}}\ket{\sigma_{j}},

so that
\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi_{B}}+\ket{\psi_{C}}+\ket{\psi_{BC}}
.

We first show the following claim:

Claim 1
.

\left\|\psi_{BC}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{p_{ind}}{\eta^{2}}
, where
\left\|\cdot\right\|
is the Euclidean Norm.

Proof.

\displaystyle p_{ind}

\displaystyle=\E_{r,r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}\braket{\psi|\left(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}}\right)|\psi}

\displaystyle=\braket{\psi|P_{B}\otimes P_{C}|\psi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\;:\;\lambda_{i}>\eta\\
j\;:\;\gamma_{j}>\eta\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\eta^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\;:\;\lambda_{i}>\eta\\
j\;:\;\gamma_{j}>\eta\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\left\|\psi_{BC}\right\|^{2}

∎

We have:

\displaystyle p_{cor}

\displaystyle=\E_{(r,r^{\prime})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left\|\left(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}}\right)\left(\ket{\psi_{B}}+\ket{\psi_{C}}+\ket{\psi_{BC}}\right)\right\|^{2}

(Cauchy-Schwartz)

\displaystyle\leq\E_{(r,r^{\prime})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}3\left(\left\|(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}})\ket{\psi_{B}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}})\ket{\psi_{C}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|(B_{r}\otimes C_{r^{\prime}})\ket{\psi_{BC}}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\E_{(r,r^{\prime})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}3\left(\left\|(B_{r}\otimes I)\ket{\psi_{B}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|(I\otimes C_{r^{\prime}})\ket{\psi_{C}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\ket{\psi_{BC}}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\E_{r,r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}3\left(\left\|(B_{r}\otimes I)\ket{\psi_{B}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|(I\otimes C_{r^{\prime}})\ket{\psi_{C}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\ket{\psi_{BC}}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=3\left(\braket{\psi_{B}|\left(P_{B}\otimes I\right)\psi_{B}}+\braket{\psi_{C}|\left(I\otimes P_{C}\right)\psi_{C}}+\left\|\psi_{BC}\right\|^{2}\right)

(
1
)

\displaystyle\leq 3\left(\eta\left(\left\|\psi_{B}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\psi_{C}\right\|^{2}\right)+\frac{p_{ind}}{\eta^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 3\left(\eta+\frac{p_{ind}}{\eta^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=6\sqrt[3]{p_{ind}}

∎

Classical Case.

We list the special case when
\mathcal{A}
in
Theorem

17
is a classical algorithm explicitly below because (1) we will need it in
Section

5.4.2
for our second application of the main theorem, and (2) we can show a quadratic (instead of cubic) loss for this special case.

Corollary 3
.

Let
P
be a non-interactive assumption and let
c:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
be a negligible non-local threshold. Then, any non-local classical
(P,\widetilde{\mathcal{D}})
-solver
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},{\sf state}_{0})
with one-shot advantage
\varepsilon=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c)}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
is a classical independent-challenge
P
-solver with advantage
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\mathsf{adv}_{P}^{(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},c)}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]=\varepsilon^{2}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Although it follows as a special (classical) case of
Theorem

17
, we will give a direct proof, as the classical concrete bound trumps the quantum bound.
We will show that if the one-shot value of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
is
\delta
, then its one-shot value in the independent-challenge setting is at least
\delta^{2}
, which suffices for the proof. The classical state
{\sf state}_{0}
can be modeled as a shared random string. We assume that
\mathcal{A}
is stateless and the general case follows by convexity.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the space of random coins used by the challenge generation algorithm
\mathsf{GenC}
of
P
. Let
p_{r}
and
q_{r}
be the probability that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
pass verification conditioned on the challenge being generated using randomness
r
, respectively. Since
\mathcal{A}
is stateless, we can write the one-shot value of
\mathcal{A}
in the independent-challenge setting as
29
29

29

This equation can be thought of as a no-signalling condition.
