\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|^{2}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}p_{r}q_{r^{\prime}}

On the other hand, the one-shot value of
\mathcal{A}
with respect to the extension
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
can be written as

\displaystyle\delta=\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r,r^{\prime}}p_{r}q_{r^{\prime}},

where
\left\{\alpha_{r,r^{\prime}}\right\}_{r,r^{\prime}}
are non-negative coefficients satisfying

\displaystyle\sum_{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r^{\prime},r}=\sum_{r^{\prime}}\alpha_{r,r^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}

for all
r\in\mathcal{R}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\delta^{2}=\sum_{r,r^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}}\alpha_{r,r^{\prime}}\alpha_{s,s^{\prime}}p_{r}q_{r^{\prime}}p_{s}q_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r,r^{\prime},s,s^{\prime}}\alpha_{r,r^{\prime}}\alpha_{s,s^{\prime}}p_{r}q_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r,s^{\prime}}p_{r}q_{s^{\prime}}\sum_{r^{\prime},s}\alpha_{r,r^{\prime}}\alpha_{s,s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|^{2}}\sum_{r,s^{\prime}}p_{r}q_{s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{\prime}

as desired.

∎

5.3.3
Main Lemma

We will extend
Lemma

10
to the non-local setting.

Lemma 19

(Main Lemma)
.

Let
V
be a classical algorithm and let
{\cal A}=\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}
be a non-local algorithm, where
\mathcal{B}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}
) acts on register
{\bf B}
(resp.,
{\bf C}
). Define the following algorithms
(\mathsf{NLValEst},\mathsf{NLRepair})
using the algorithms
\mathsf{ValEst}
and
\mathsf{Repair}
described in
Lemma

10
:

•

(\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\rho,1^{1/\varepsilon})
: compute
\left(\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\cdot,1^{1/(\varepsilon/2)})\otimes\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{C}}(\cdot,1^{1/(\varepsilon/2)})\right)(\rho_{{\bf B}{\bf C}})
to obtain
(p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}})
(
\mathcal{B}
’s output),
p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}
(
\mathcal{C}
’s output) and
\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}}
is the joint output of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, where
\mathcal{B}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}
) output the register
{\bf B}
(resp.,
{\bf C}
).

•

\sigma^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NLRepair}_{V,{\cal A},\Pi_{\mathcal{B}},\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\sigma_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},y_{\mathcal{B}},p_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}},p_{\mathcal{C}},1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{k}\right)
: for k-outcome projections
\Pi_{\mathcal{B}},\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}
on register
{\bf B},{\bf C}
, respectively, compute the following:

\left(\mathsf{Repair}_{V,\mathcal{B},\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}}\left(\cdot,y_{\mathcal{B}},p_{\mathcal{B}},1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{k}\right)\otimes\mathsf{Repair}_{V,\mathcal{C},\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\cdot,y_{\mathcal{C}},p_{\mathcal{C}},1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{k})\right)\right)(\sigma_{{\bf B}{\bf C}})

Denote the result by
\sigma^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}}
.

Note that
(\mathsf{NLValEst},\mathsf{NLRepair})
are efficient
30
30

30

Here, by efficient we mean that the runtime of
\mathsf{NLValEst}
is polynomial in the size of
\rho
,
1/\varepsilon
, the runtime of
V
and the runtime of
\mathcal{A}
. Similarly for
\mathsf{NLRepair}
.
algorithms given that
(\mathsf{ValEst},\mathsf{Repair})
are efficient algorithms by
Lemma

10
. In addition, we have the following guarantees:

1.

Value Estimation:
For any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\E_{(\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\mathbf{\rho}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},1^{1/\varepsilon})}\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\right]=\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}V(y_{\mathcal{B}};r_{\mathcal{B}})=1\\
\land\\
V(y_{\mathcal{C}};r_{\mathcal{C}})=1\end{subarray}\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}}\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\\
(y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathcal{B}(\cdot,r_{\mathcal{B}})\otimes\mathcal{C}(\cdot,r_{\mathcal{C}})(\mathbf{\rho}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}})\end{subarray}\right].

(5)

2.

Almost-Projective Estimation:
For any
\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}-p_{\mathcal{B}}^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\ \lor\ \left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-p_{\mathcal{C}}^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}(\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\mathbf{\rho}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},1^{1/\varepsilon})\\
(\rho^{**}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},p^{**}_{\mathcal{B}},p^{**}_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\mathbf{\rho}^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},1^{1/\varepsilon^{\prime}})\end{subarray}\right]\leq\varepsilon.

3.

Repairing
: For any state
\mathbf{\rho}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}}
and any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}-p_{\mathcal{B}}^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\ \lor\ \left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-p_{\mathcal{C}}^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\ :\ \begin{subarray}{c}(\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\mathbf{\rho}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},1^{1/\varepsilon})\\
\ \\
(\sigma_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow(\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\Pi_{\mathcal{C}})(\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}})\\
\ \\
\sigma^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{NLRepair}_{V,{\cal A},\Pi_{\mathcal{B}},\Pi_{\mathcal{C}}}\left(\sigma_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},y_{\mathcal{B}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}},1^{1/\varepsilon},1^{k}\right)\\
\ \\
(\rho^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{B}},p^{*}_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\mathbf{\rho}^{*}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}},1^{1/\varepsilon})\end{subarray}\right]\leq\varepsilon.

(6)

Proof.

We start with value estimation. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{0}=(\Pi_{A},I-\Pi_{A})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=(\Pi_{B},I-\Pi_{B})
be the projective measurements applied by
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\cdot,1^{\varepsilon/2})
. Using
Lemma

5
, we decompose the
{\bf B}
register into Jordan subspaces as
\mathcal{H}_{\bf B}=\bigoplus S_{j}
. Let
\left\{\ket{v^{A}_{j,1}},\ket{v^{A}_{j,0}}\right\}
and
\left\{\ket{v^{B}_{j,1}},\ket{v^{B}_{j,0}}\right\}
be the orthogonal eigenbases of
S_{j}
with respect to
\Pi_{A},\Pi_{B}
.

Similarly for
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{C}}(\cdot,1^{\varepsilon/2})
, we consider projective measurements
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}=(\Gamma_{A},I-\Gamma_{A})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=(\Gamma_{B},I-\Gamma_{B})
, with the Jordan decomposition
\mathcal{H}_{\bf C}=\bigoplus T_{j^{\prime}}
and the eigenbases
\left\{\ket{w^{A}_{j^{\prime},1}},\ket{w^{A}_{j^{\prime},0}}\right\}
and
\left\{\ket{w^{B}_{j^{\prime},1}},\ket{w^{B}_{j^{\prime},0}}\right\}
. For simplicity, we will assume
\dim S_{j}=\dim T_{j^{\prime}}=2
for all
j,j^{\prime}
. The degenerate cases can be handled similarly
31
31

31

See
[
CMSZ21
]
Section 4.1 to see how to handle degenerate subspaces generically.
.

We will need the following claim:

Claim 2
.

Let
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}=\left\{\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{{\bf B},j}\right\}_{j}
be the jordan subspace measurement, with
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{{\bf B},j}=\ket{v^{A}_{j,0}}\!\!\bra{v^{A}_{j,0}}+\ket{v^{A}_{j,1}}\!\!\bra{v^{A}_{j,1}}
. Similarly define
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf C}
. Then,
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}
commutes with
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\cdot,1^{\varepsilon/2})
. Similarly,
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf C}
commutes with
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{C}}(\cdot,1^{\varepsilon/2})
.

Proof.

We will show that
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}
commutes with
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\cdot,1^{\varepsilon/2})
. Recall that
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}
commutes with both
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
. We write the register
{\bf B}
as two registers,
{\bf P}
and
{\bf Q}
, where
{\bf P}
stores the input state and
{\bf Q}
contains all auxiliary registers used in the computation. In particular, this means that the measurements
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{0},\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{0},\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}
are all restricted to the
{\bf P}
register. By
Lemma

10
bullet 3, every step of
\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\cdot,1^{\varepsilon/2})
falls into one of the following categories:

1.

Apply
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{b}
on the
{\bf P}
register conditioned on a qubit located in the
{\bf Q}
register for some
b\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. This captures all steps adaptively applying a projective measurement
\mathcal{M}^{\mathcal{B}}_{b}
.

2.

Apply a local operation on the
{\bf Q}
register. This captures the steps computing the database of outcomes and the functions
f,g
described in
Lemma

10
.

Because
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}
is a projection applied to the
{\bf P}
register alone, it commutes with operations from both categories above.
∎

Without loss of generality assume that
\rho
is a pure state, for the mixed state case follows by convexity. By assumption, the bipartite state after steps (a) and (b) described in the 2-Projection Implementation property in
Lemma

10
are performed on both registers has the form

\displaystyle\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}\ket{v^{B}_{j,1}}\ket{w^{B}_{j^{\prime},1}}

Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{ValEst}}
denote the execution of
\mathsf{ValEst}
after steps (a) and (b) mentioned above. By
2
, the product Jordan subspace measurement
\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf B}\otimes\Pi^{\mathsf{Jor}}_{\bf C}
commutes with
\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,\mathcal{A}}(\cdot,1^{1/\varepsilon})
, hence the values
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
are distributed as follows:

•

Sample
(j,j^{\prime})
with probability
|\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}|^{2}
,

•

Compute
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{ValEst}}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\ket{v^{B}_{j,1}},1^{1/(\varepsilon/2)})
and
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{ValEst}}_{V,\mathcal{C}}(\ket{v^{B}_{j^{\prime},1}},1^{1/(\varepsilon/2)})
,

•

Output
(p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})
.

Let
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}
be the value of state
\ket{v^{B}_{j,1}}
as defined on the RHS of
eq.

3
. By the same equation, we know that
\E\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}|j\right]=p_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}
. Similarly define
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{j^{\prime}}
so that
\E\left[p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}|j^{\prime}\right]=p_{\mathcal{B}}^{j^{\prime}}
. Let
p^{j,j^{\prime}}
denote the value of state
\ket{v^{B}_{j,1}}\ket{v^{C}_{j^{\prime},1}}
as defined on the RHS of
eq.

5
. Because this is pure product state, we have that
p^{j,j^{\prime}}=p_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{j^{\prime}}
, and also that the variables
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
are independent conditioned on
j,j^{\prime}
. On the other hand, since computing the value commutes with the Jordan measurements, the value
p
for the state
\rho
is distributed as follows:

•

Sample
(j,j^{\prime})
with probability
|\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}|^{2}
,

•

Output
p^{j,j^{\prime}}
.

Therefore, putting everything together, we have

\displaystyle\E\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}\right|^{2}\E\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}|j,j^{\prime}\right]=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}\right|^{2}\E\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}|j\right]\cdot\E\left[p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}|j^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}\right|^{2}p_{\mathcal{B}}^{j}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{j^{\prime}}=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{j,j^{\prime}}\right|^{2}p^{j,j^{\prime}}=p

as desired. Next, we show almost-projective estimation. Recall that
\mathsf{NLValEst}_{V,{\cal A}}(\cdot,1^{1/\varepsilon})
applies

\left(\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{B}}(\cdot,1^{1/(\varepsilon/2)})\otimes\mathsf{ValEst}_{V,\mathcal{C}}(\cdot,1^{1/(\varepsilon/2)})\right),

and the operations on the
{\bf B},{\bf C}
registers commute. Therefore, using condition (2) of
Lemma

10
for the state
\mathsf{Tr}_{{\bf C}}\left(\mathbf{\rho}_{{\bf B}{\bf C}}\right)
, we have
\Pr\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}-p_{\mathcal{B}}^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\right]\leq\varepsilon/2
, and similarly
\Pr\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-p_{\mathcal{C}}^{**}\right|\geq\varepsilon\right]\leq\varepsilon/2
. Using a union bound, we achieve the desired property. The repairing property follows by a similar argument, after observing that the procedure in
eq.

6
involves two parallel executions of
eq.

4
on registers
{\bf B}
and
{\bf C}
.
∎

5.3.4
Achieving Non-Local Persistence

In Theorem 4.1 of
[
BBK22
]
, the output
p^{*}
of the value estimation
\mathsf{ValEst}
estimates the value of the solver in expectation, and can be used to achieve a distribution over
(p^{*},\eta)
-persistent solvers. We observe that the estimation
p^{*}
could be stored non-locally
(p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})
such that
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}=p^{*}
. These local values are enough for non-local state repair by
Lemma

19
, and using this the proof of
[
BBK22
]
can be straightforwardly applied to the non-local setting. We state the formal theorem statement below.

Theorem 20

(Non-Local Persistence Theorem)
.

Let
P
be a non-interactive falsifiable assumption with a verifiably polynomial image. Let
\eta=\frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}}
. There exist efficient non-local quantum algorithms
S=S_{1}\otimes S_{2},R=R_{1}\otimes R_{2}
with the following syntax and guarantee:

•

S^{\mathcal{A}}({\sf state}_{0})=\left(S_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}\otimes S_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}\right)({\sf state}_{0})
takes as input a non-local algorithm
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
and a bipartite state
{\sf state}_{0}
. It outputs a state
{\sf state}_{0}^{*}
and values
(p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})
, where
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
is output by
S_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}
and
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
is output by
S_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}
.

•

R^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{B}},x_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}_{i-1}^{*})=\left(R_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{B}},\cdot)\otimes R_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{C}},\cdot)\right)({\sf state}_{i-1}^{*})
takes as input a non-local algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, a security parameter
\lambda
, a step
i
, a pair of inputs
(x_{\mathcal{B}},x_{\mathcal{C}})
, and a bipartite state
{\sf state}_{i-1}^{*}
. It outputs a pair of solutions
(y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}})
, where
y_{\mathcal{B}}
is output by
R_{2}^{\mathcal{B}}
and
y_{\mathcal{C}}
is output by
R_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}
, and a bipartite state
{\sf state}_{i}^{*}
.

For any non-local independent-challenge
P
-solver
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},{\sf state}_{0})
with one-shot value
p=\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[\mathcal{A}\right]
, with
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}}
, considering the random variable
({\sf state}_{0}^{*},p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})\leftarrow S^{\mathcal{A}}({\sf state}_{0})
, we have:

1.

\E\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\right]=p

2.

\mathcal{R}^{*}=(R^{\mathcal{A}},{\sf state}_{0}^{*})
sampled in this process is a distribution over efficient stateful non-local solvers that is
(p_{B}^{*}p_{C}^{*},\eta)
-persistent.

Moreover, if
P
does not have a verifiably polynomial image, the same holds, but
S
is not efficient.

Proof.

The proof will closely follow the proof of Theorem 4.1 in
[
BBK22
]
. Let
P=(\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver},c)
with polynomial-image verifier
K
and polynomial-image bound
k
. Let
\eta=1/\mathsf{poly}(\cdot)
. We will denote by
{\bf B},{\bf C}
the two registers for the non-local algorithms
S,R,\mathcal{A}
. For
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
, we define the
(k+1)
-outcome projective measurement
\Pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{x}
as follows:

•

Coherently run
\mathcal{B}
with input
x
.

•

First, measure if the output of
\mathcal{B}
is in the polynomial image specified by
K
.

–

If not, output
\bot

–

If yes, measure the output of
\mathcal{B}
and output the answer
y\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{m}
.

•

Uncompute.

We similarly define the projective measurement
\Pi^{\mathcal{C}}_{x}
. We also define the wrapper non-local solver
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}
(resp.,
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
) takes as input
r\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{d}
, computes
x=\mathsf{GenC}(1^{\lambda};r)
and runs
\mathcal{B}
(resp.,
{\mathcal{C}}
) on input
x
, outputting the result
y\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{m}
. Next, we move on to describe
S^{\mathcal{A}}
and
R^{\mathcal{A}}
next.

Description of
S^{\mathcal{A}}
:

On input
{\sf state}_{0}
, compute
({\sf state}_{0}^{*},p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}({\sf state}_{0},1^{10/\eta})
. That is,
S_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}
runs
\mathsf{ValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}}(\cdot,1^{20/\eta})
and
S_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}
runs
\mathsf{ValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(\cdot,1^{20/\eta})
. Output
({\sf state}_{0}^{*},p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})
.

Description of
R^{\mathcal{A}}
:

On input
(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{B}},x_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}_{i-1}^{*})
, where
R_{1}
receives
1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{B}}
and the register
{\bf B}
while
R_{2}
receives
1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\bf C}
, do the following:

•

Define
\varepsilon_{i}=\eta/32i^{2}
, so that
(\varepsilon_{i})_{i}
is monotonically decreasing and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\varepsilon_{i}<\eta/16
.

•

Compute
(\sigma_{i-1},q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1},q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}({\sf state}_{i-1}^{*},1^{1/\varepsilon_{i}})
.

•

Compute
(y^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},\sigma_{i}^{*})\leftarrow(\Pi_{x_{\mathcal{B}}}^{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\Pi_{x_{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{C}})(\sigma_{i-1})
, where
y_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{m}\cup\left\{\bot\right\}
and
\sigma_{i}^{*}
is the post-measurement state.

•

Compute
\rho_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{NLRepair}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\Pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{x_{\mathcal{B}}},\Pi^{\mathcal{C}}_{x_{\mathcal{C}}}}(\sigma_{i}^{*},y^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1},1^{1/\varepsilon_{i},1^{k+1}})
.

•

Compute
({\sf state}_{i}^{*},p^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}(\rho_{i},1^{1/\varepsilon_{i}})
.

•

Output
(y_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}},{\sf state}_{i}^{*})
, where
y_{\mathcal{B}}
, register
{\bf B}
is
R_{1}
’s output while
y_{\mathcal{C}}
, register
{\bf C}
is
R_{2}
’s output.

Note that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are defined implicitly above given that every step is a non-local algorithm;
R_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}
performs the operations on the
{\bf B}
register, and
R_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}
on the
{\bf C}
register.

Consider a solver-aided non-local algorithm
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}
with input
z
, and the random variables sampled by the process below:

•

(p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*},{\sf state}_{0}^{*})\leftarrow S^{\mathcal{A}}({\sf state}_{0})
. Equivalently,
({\sf state}_{0}^{*},p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}({\sf state}_{0},1^{2/\eta})
.

•

For
i\geq 1
,
(y^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},{\sf state}_{i}^{*})\leftarrow R^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{i},x_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},x_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},{\sf state}_{i-1}^{*})
, where
(x_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},x_{\mathcal{C}}^{i})
is the
i
th pair of inputs chosen by
\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}
. In more detail,

–

(\sigma_{i-1},q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1},q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}({\sf state}_{i-1}^{*},1^{1/\varepsilon_{i}})
.

–

(y^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},\sigma_{i}^{*})\leftarrow(\Pi_{x_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}}^{\mathcal{B}}\otimes\Pi_{x_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}}^{\mathcal{C}})(\sigma_{i-1})
.

–

\rho_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{NLRepair}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},\Pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{x_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}},\Pi^{\mathcal{C}}_{x_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}}}(\sigma_{i}^{*},y^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1},1^{1/\varepsilon_{i},1^{k+1}})
.

–

({\sf state}_{i}^{*},p^{i}_{\mathcal{B}},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{NLValEst}_{\mathsf{Ver},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}(\rho_{i},1^{1/\varepsilon_{i}})
.

Over the randomness of this process, we have the following identities:

\displaystyle\E\left[p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\right]=p

(7)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{B}}^{0}\right|\geq\eta/10\lor\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}\right|\geq\eta/10\right]\leq\eta/10

(8)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}-q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}\right|\geq\varepsilon_{i}\lor\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\right|\geq\varepsilon_{i}\right]\leq\varepsilon_{i},\quad\forall i\geq 1

(9)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1}-p_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}\right|\geq\varepsilon_{i}\lor\left|q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1}-p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\right|\geq\varepsilon_{i}\right]\leq\varepsilon_{i},\quad\forall i\geq 1

(10)

\displaystyle\E\left[q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1}q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1}\right]=\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i-1,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}\right],\quad\forall i\geq 1

(11)

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1}-\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i-1,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}\right]\right|\geq\varepsilon_{i}\right]\leq\varepsilon_{i},\quad\forall i\geq 1,\mathcal{S},z

(12)

Equation

7
follows from the value estimation property in
Lemma

19
and the fact that
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is a wrapper solver for
\mathcal{A}
. This is the first bullet we need to show to prove the theorem.

Moving on to the second bullet (persistence),
eqs.

8
and
9
follow from the almost-projective estimation property in
Lemma

19
since
\varepsilon\leq\eta/2
and
\varepsilon_{i+1}\leq\varepsilon_{i}
.
Equation

10
follows from the repairing property. Finally,
eq.

12
follows from the value estimation property and from the fact that
32
32

32

Here we define
\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},r,\bot)=0
for convenience.

\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i-1,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}\right]=\Pr\left[\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},r_{\mathcal{B}}^{i},y_{\mathcal{B}}^{i})=\mathsf{Ver}(1^{\lambda},r_{\mathcal{C}}^{i},y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i})=1\right]
. The latter is true because
y_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}
(resp.,
y_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}
) is distributed the same as the output of
\mathcal{B}
(resp.,
\mathcal{C}
) conditioned on
y_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}\neq\bot
, and otherwise the verification fails as expected.

Therefore, by union bound we have with probability at least
1-\eta/10-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}4\varepsilon_{i}\geq 1-\eta
that

1.

\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{B}}^{0}\right|\leq\eta/10
and
\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{0}\right|\leq\eta/10
,

2.

\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}-q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{i}
and
\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{i}
,

3.

\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}-q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{i}
and
\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1}\right|\leq\varepsilon_{i}
,

4.

\E\left[q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i-1}q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i-1}\right]=\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i-1,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}\right]

for all
i\geq 1
. Conditioned on the inequalities 1-4 above being true, we will show that

\displaystyle\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-\mathsf{val}_{P}^{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}}\left[i,\mathcal{S}_{z}^{\mathcal{R}^{*}}\right]\right|\leq\eta

(13)

to finish the proof. Indeed, by triangle inequality we have that
\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{B}}^{i}\right|\leq\eta/10+\sum_{j=1}^{i}2\varepsilon_{j}\leq\eta/3
, and similarly
\left|p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\right|\leq\eta/3
. Since
p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*},p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}\in[0,1]
, this implies that
\left|p_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}-q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}q_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}\right|\leq 2(\eta/3)+(\eta/3)^{2}\leq\eta
. Taking the expectation, we obtain
eq.

13
.

If
P
does not have a verifiably polynomial image, note that we can always take
k=2^{n}
, i.e.
P
always has a verifiably
exponential
image. Thus, the proof remains the same, except the runtime of
R
now depends exponentially on
n
.

∎

Remark 7
.

We note that the transformation in
Theorem

20
is not optimal in terms of concrete efficiency. For instance, it is redundant to apply value estimation once in
S
and twice in
R
. The reason we do this is to use previous work as closely to a black-box as possible, thus keeping the proof simple.

5.3.5
Non-Local Classical-to-Quantum Lifting Theorem

The remaining steps to reach our non-local lifting theorem (
Theorem

22
) are essentially the same as those in
[
BBK22
]
. For this reason, we do not repeat the detailed proofs here. Nonetheless, we note that the proof has the following (informal) structure: given a
P
-solver,

1.

Obtain a persistent
P
-solver.

2.

Next, obtain a memoryless
P
-solver, i.e. one that only remembers the number of times it has interacted with the assumption.

3.

Next, obtain a stateless solver.

Step
1
corresponds to
Theorem

20
, whereas steps
2
,
3
can be summarized below:

Theorem 21
.

There exists an efficient non-local quantum algorithm
\mathsf{Sim}=\mathsf{Sim}_{1}\otimes\mathsf{Sim}_{2}
with the following properties. Let
\mathcal{A}=(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C},{\sf state}_{0})
be a
(p,\eta)
-persistent
(\ell,\ell_{\mathcal{B}},\ell_{\mathcal{C}})
-stateful non-local independent-challenge
P
-solver for a falsifiable non-interactive assumption
P
. Let
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\vec{x}_{\mathcal{C}}
each be (independently) sampled from an efficiently samplable distribution over
k
-tuples of
P
-instances and define
\vec{x}:=(\vec{x}_{\mathcal{B}},\vec{x}_{\mathcal{C}})
. Then, there exists a
(p,\eta)
-persistent distribution
\left\{\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}=(\mathcal{B}^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha})\right\}_{\alpha}
over stateless solvers such that

\mathsf{Sim}^{\mathcal{A}}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell},1^{1/\delta},\vec{x}^{*})=\mathsf{Sim}_{1}^{\mathcal{B}}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell_{\mathcal{B}}},1^{1/\delta},\vec{x_{\mathcal{B}}}^{*})\otimes\mathsf{Sim}_{2}^{\mathcal{C}}(1^{\lambda},1^{\ell_{\mathcal{C}}},1^{1/\delta},\vec{x_{\mathcal{C}}}^{*})

makes non-adaptive black-box queries to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
and produces a distribution within
\delta
statistical distance from
\mathcal{A}^{\alpha}(1^{\lambda},\vec{x})
.

Proof (sketch).

Follows from Corollary 6.2 in
[
BBK22
]
after making the observation that the simulators in Theorem 5.1 and Theorem 6.1 both preserve non-locality, i.e. they are non-local simulators if given as input non-local solvers.
∎

The theorem below is a direct consequence of
Theorems

20
and
21
as per Theorem 7.1 of
[
BBK22
]
. We refer the reader to
[
BBK22
]
for the proof.

Theorem 22

(Non-Local Classical-to-Quantum Lifting)
.

Let
P=(\mathsf{GenC}_{P},\mathsf{Ver}_{P},c_{P})
and
Q=(\mathsf{GenC}_{Q},\mathsf{Ver}_{Q},c_{Q})
be non-interactive assumptions. Assume there exists a non-adaptive non-local classical black-box
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q})
-reduction from solving
Q
to solving
P
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P}=\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}^{d_{P}}\times\left\{0,1\right\}^{d_{P}}}
is the uniform extension. Then, there exists an inefficient non-local quantum
(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{P},c_{P},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{Q},c_{Q})
-reduction from solving
Q
to solving
P
. If
P
has verifiably polynomial image, then the reduction is efficient.

5.4
Applications

In this section, we present two applications of our main theorem (
Theorems

13
and
14
). The first one is information theoretic single-decryptor encryption for one-bit messages, which relies on simultaneous quantum Goldreich-Levin extraction. The second application involves the effect of changing the challenge distribution in a cloning game to unclonable security. Informally, we show that independent-challenge unclonable security implies unclonable security for correlated distributions for primitives which have negligible trivial success probability.

5.4.1
Single-Decryptor Encryption

In this section, we analyze a corollary of
Theorem

14
, which is Simultaneous Quantum Goldreich-Levin Lemma. Then, we show a construction of information theoretic single-decryptor encryption as a corollary.

Definition 33

(Simultaneous Extraction)
.

We say that an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
can
simultaneously extract
a classical function
f(k,x,r)
given a quantum token
\rho_{k,x}
using key
k=(k_{B},k_{C})
with probability
\delta
if
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in the following experiment with probability
\delta
:

•

In phase 1, the challenger sends
\rho_{k,x}
to
\mathcal{A}
, who applies a CPTP map to split the state into two registers
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
.

•

In phase 2,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
can no longer communicate. The challenger samples independent random coins
(r,r^{\prime})\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}
, then sends
(r,k_{B})
to
\mathcal{B}
and
(r^{\prime},k_{C})
to
\mathcal{C}
. Later,
\mathcal{B}
outputs a string
z_{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
outputs a string
z_{C}
.

•

The adversary wins if
z_{B}=f(k,x,r)
and
z_{C}=f(k,x,r^{\prime})
.

It is understood that
x
above is a random variable.

Lemma 23

(Simultaneous Quantum Goldreich-Levin)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{R}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and there exists a (possibly inefficient) adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which can simultaneously extract
\langle r,x\rangle
from a quantum token
\rho_{k,x}
using key
k
, with probability
1/2+\varepsilon
for a non-negligible function
\varepsilon
. Then, there exists an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
which can simultaneously extract
33
33

33

We note that the values
(r,r^{\prime})
can be ignored when extracting
x
.

x
given the same token
\rho_{k,x}
using key
k
with non-negligible probability
\varepsilon^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We will interpret the key
k=(k_{B},k_{C})=:(\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}})
as the challenge and
x=:m
as the message in a cloning game. We will assume here that
k_{B},k_{C}
are independently distributed, which is sufficient for the application to single-decryptor encryption. See
Appendix

A
for a direct proof
34
34

34

The direct proof also shows that if the simultaneous extractor for
\langle r,x\rangle
is efficient, then so is the simultaneous extractor for
x
.
of
Lemma

23
which covers the general case. Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
be a (stateful) cloning game, such that:

•

\rho_{k,x}
is the token received by
\mathcal{A}
,
x
is the message, and
k
is the challenge received by
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk},x,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})
accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}=x
, i.e.
\mathcal{G}
is a search game.

Now define a new cloning game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
, where:

•

\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},x)
computes
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},x)
and samples
r\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{R}}
. It outputs
(r,\mathsf{ch})
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},x,(r,\mathsf{ch}),\mathsf{ans})
accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}=\langle r,x\rangle
.

Suppose that the distribution of
x
, denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
, is non-trivial in the sense that
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}})\leq 1/2+\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
-evasive, for otherwise the proof is trivial.

We will use the contrapositive of
Theorem

14
, with and
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}=\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathsf{ind}}
. Now if
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
exists, then
\mathcal{G}
does not have information theoretic
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
independent-challenge unclonable security, where
\mathcal{M}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
is the message space and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
denotes the distribution of
x
. Now we observe that the conditions of
Theorem

14
, i.e. conditions in
items

(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
and
item

(b)
of
Theorem

13
, are all satisfied. Indeed,
items

(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
are satisfied by assumption.
Item

(b)
follows from the well-known classical local Goldreich-Levin extraction
35
35

35

Note that even though the success probability of the extractor may depend on
x
, its description does not.

[
GL89
]
. Therefore, it follows that
\mathcal{G}
does not have
(\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
information theoretic independent-challenge unclonable security, meaning there exists
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
which can simultaneously extract
x
with non-negligible probability as desired.

∎

Search to Decision Transformation.

Corollary 4

(Search to Decision)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning search game with extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
such that (1)
\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
both do not depend on
m
, (2)
\mathcal{G}
has statistical correctness, and (3)
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon,\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
unclonable security. Define a (stateful) cloning decision game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
as follows:

•

There is no message, i.e.
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\left\{\bot\right\}
is the message space.

•

\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda})
computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=\mathsf{sk}
.

•

\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=\mathsf{sk}
, samples
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
, and computes
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
. It outputs a token
\rho
and random coins
r_{\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}}=m

•

\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime};r_{\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=\mathsf{sk}
. It interprets the random coins as
r_{\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}}=(r,b,r_{\mathsf{GenC}})
, where
r\in\mathcal{M}
,
b\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, and
r_{\mathsf{GenC}}
is randomness for
\mathsf{GenC}
. Then it computes
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m;r_{\mathsf{GenC}})
, and outputs
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}=(r,\langle r,m\rangle\oplus b,\mathsf{ch})
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\mathsf{ch}^{\prime},\mathsf{ans}^{\prime},r_{\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}})
parses the input as
r_{\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}}=(r,b,r_{\mathsf{GenC}})
and outputs
b^{\prime}=[\mathsf{ans}^{\prime}==b]
.

Then,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has statistical correctness and
(\mathcal{D}_{\bot},\sqrt{\varepsilon}/2,\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}^{\prime})
independent-challenge unclonable security, where the extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk},m^{\prime})
is defined as follows:

1.

Run
(r_{B},r_{C})\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)

2.

Sample
r_{B}^{\prime},r_{C}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{M}
and
b_{B},b_{C}\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}

3.

Output
\left((r_{B}^{\prime},b_{B},r_{B}),(r_{C}^{\prime},b_{C},r_{C})\right)
.

In particular, if
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
independent-challenge unclonable security, then
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has
(\mathcal{D}_{\bot},\sqrt{\varepsilon}/2)
independent-challenge unclonable security.

Proof.

We construct the game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
as follows:

We define several hybrids:

•

Hybrid 0:
This is the cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{\bot}}
. Since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a decision game, we have
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}^{\prime})\geq 1/2
. Assume for the sake of contradiction that the statement is false, then there exists a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{C}_{0}\right)
that succeeds in this experiment with probability
p>\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}/2
.

•

Hybrid 1:
In this hybrid, we modify the success condition for the adversary. Instead of outputting the bits
b_{B}
and
b_{C}
,
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are now required to output
\langle r,m\rangle
and
\langle r^{\prime},m\rangle
, respectively, where
(r,r^{\prime})
are the random coins generated for
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, respectively. This hybrid is clearly equivalent to Hybrid 0, since
\langle r,m\rangle\oplus b_{B}
and
\langle r^{\prime},m\rangle\oplus b_{C}
are known to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, respectively. Thus, there exists
\left(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)
which succeeds in this hybrid with probability
p
. Specifically,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
simply run
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{C}_{0}
; then they XOR the output with the value mentioned above.

•

Hybrid 2:
In this hybrid, we truncate the challenges given to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. Specifically, instead of getting
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}=(r,\langle r,m\rangle\oplus b_{B},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}})
,
\mathcal{B}
will receive
(r,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}})
; similarly
\mathcal{C}
will receive
(r^{\prime},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}})
. This hybrid is equivalent to Hybrid 1, so there exists an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}\right)
which succeeds in this hybrid with probability
p
. The reason is that
b_{B},b_{C}
are uniformly random bits that information theoretically hide the inner products
\langle r,m\rangle
and
\langle r^{\prime},m\rangle
. In more detail,
\left(\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}\right)
can be constructed as follows:

–

Upon receiving a token
\rho
,
\mathcal{A}_{2}
runs
\rho_{BC}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}
and samples random bits
b_{B}^{\prime},b_{C}^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}
. Then it sends the bipartite state
\rho_{BC}\otimes\ket{b_{B}^{\prime}}\!\!\bra{b_{B}^{\prime}}_{B}\otimes\ket{b_{C}}\!\!\bra{b_{C}}_{C}
to
\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

–

\mathcal{B}_{2}
, upon receiving
(r,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}})
from the challenger and a state
\rho_{B}\otimes\ket{b_{B}^{\prime}}\!\!\bra{b_{B}^{\prime}}_{B}
from
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, runs
\mathcal{B}_{1}
with input
(r,b_{B}^{\prime},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}})
.
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is defined similarly.

Since the view of
\left(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)
exactly matches Hybrid 1, we conclude that
\left(\mathcal{A}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}\right)
succeeds in Hybrid 2 with probability
p>\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}/2
.

By
Lemma

23
, this implies that there exists a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
which succeeds in
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}
with probability greater than
4(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/2)^{2}=\varepsilon
, a contradiction.

∎

By plugging in
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84}}
from
Definition

21
, we get the following corollary:

Corollary 5
.

There exists a single-decryptor encryption scheme in the plain model with information-theoretic independent-challenge security.

Proof.

It could easily be checked that the resulting game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the cloning game corresponding to the following single-decryptor encryption scheme
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
:

1.

(\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda}))
: Sample
m,\theta\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
. Output
\mathsf{sk}=(m,\theta)

2.

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk})
: Parse the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(m,\theta)
and output the decryption token
\ket{m^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{m^{\theta}}
.

3.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sk},b)
: Sample
r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
. Output
ct=(r,\langle r,m\rangle\oplus b,\theta)

4.

\mathsf{Dec}(\rho,ct)
: Parse
ct=(r,b^{\prime},\theta)
. Measure
\rho
in basis
H^{\theta}
to get
m
. Output
\langle r,m\rangle\oplus b^{\prime}
.

Note that since
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84}}
is a cloning encryption game, it is secure against any extension, including the independent-challenge case. Therefore, the single-decryptor scheme above is optimally secure against independent challenges by
Corollary

4
.

∎

5.4.2
Relationship Between Challenge Distributions

As a second application, we show that when the trivial success probability of a cloning game is negligible, unclonable security for independent challenges implies unclonable security for any challenge distribution, up to a polynomial loss in the success probability.

Corollary 6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a
\mathcal{D}
-evasive cloning game with
(\mathcal{D},\mathsf{negl})
independent-challenge unclonable security. Suppose that for any key-message pair
(\mathsf{sk},m)
, the non-interactive assumption induced by
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},\mathsf{sk},m)
has verifiably polynomial image. Then,
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\mathsf{negl},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
unclonable security for any extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\mathsf{negl})
identical-challenge unclonable security.

Proof.

Follows directly from
Theorem

13
by setting
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. The only non-trivial condition is
(b)
, which follows by
Corollary

3
. Note that the classical reduction is the identity reduction, hence it does not depend on
(\mathsf{sk},m)
.
∎

Remark 8
.

The corollary above can be applied to any unclonable primitive with verifiably polynomial image, where
p^{\mathsf{triv}}
is negligible, including copy-protection for functions with output-length
\omega(\log\lambda)
.

Direct Proof with Concrete Bounds.

We can in fact show a stronger statement without using the main theorem, namely that the identity reduction works in
Corollary

6
with a cubic loss in success probability.

Alternate proof of
Corollary

6
.

Follows directly from
Lemma

18
after setting
\mathcal{D}
in the lemma statement to be the output of
\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}
, setting
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
to be the output of the extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
, and setting
B_{r}
(
C_{r}
) to be a POVM element which tests whether
\mathcal{B}
(
\mathcal{C}
) passes the verification
\mathsf{Ver}
on challenge
r
.
∎

6
Relating Unclonable-Search and Unclonable-Indistinguishability

In this section, we will give a relationship between games satisfying unclonable-indistinguishability security
Definition

11
and unclonable-search security
Definition

10
.

In
Section

6.1
, we show that games with unclonable-search property imply games satisfying unclonable-indistinguishability property. In
Section

6.2
, we show that games with unclonable-indistinguishability property imply games satisfying unclonable-search property.

We start with some simple observations before moving onto our results.

Trivial Success.

We show upper and lower bounds for trivial success probability of search games.

Lemma 24

(Trivial Success Probability of Search Games)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning search game with correctness
\delta
and let
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
be an extension. Then,

\displaystyle(1-\sqrt{1-\delta})\left(\mathsf{OPT}(m\mid\mathsf{ch})-\sqrt{1-\delta}\right)\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})\leq\mathsf{OPT}(m\mid\mathsf{ch}).

where the variables
(m,\mathsf{ch})
are sampled as in
Definition

2
.

Proof Sketch.

Consider a
\mathcal{B}
-trivial attack. For the upper-bound, simply bound the success probability of
\mathcal{B}
. For the lower bound, note that for
(1-\sqrt{1-\sigma})
fraction of the time,
\mathcal{C}
will succeed with probability at least
1-\sqrt{1-\sigma}
by correctness. Observe that
\mathcal{B}
can succeed with probability
\mathsf{OPT}(m\mid\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{aux})
without the token. Use union bound to complete the proof. Similar for the
\mathcal{C}
-trivial attack.
∎

Corollary 7
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a search game with perfect correctness, then
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})=\mathsf{OPT}(m\mid\mathsf{ch})
.

Message Hiding.

Clearly, a search game must have the property that the token hides the message, for otherwise cloning would be trivial. We formalize this in the lemma below.

Lemma 25

(Message Hiding)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning search game with
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security, then for any QPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[m\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(1^{\lambda},\rho)\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\\
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)\end{subarray}\right]\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)+\varepsilon(\lambda)

Proof.

If the statement is false, then
\mathcal{A}
can send
m
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
in the splitting phase, both of which output
m
as their answer, hence
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
breaks the
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security of
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

6.1
Search-to-Indistinguishability

We transform games satisfying unclonable-search property to games satisfying unclonable-indistinguishability property in the following steps.

1.

In
Section

6.1.1
, we present a generic transformation that reduces the unclonable-search security of cloning games, where the message distribution comes from a high entropy distribution, to unclonable-search security, where the message distribution is uniform.

2.

In
Section

6.1.2
, we consider a new notion of security called augmented unclonable security. In this security notion, all the adversaries have oracle access to a point function
P_{m}(\cdot)
, where
m
is such that the adversary receives a token generated using
m
. We show that we can generically transform any game satisfying unclonable-search security into one satisfying augmented unclonable-search security.

3.

In
Section

6.1.3
, we show how augmented unclonable security implies unclonable-indistinguishability security.

6.1.1
Relationship Between Message Distributions

The following lemma is adapted from Theorem 9 in
[
BL20
]
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning game. Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over the message space
\mathcal{M}
with min-entropy
h
, then for any cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
and any extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
of
\mathsf{GenC}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq 2^{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|-h}\cdot\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right].

Proof.

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\Pr\left[m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\right]\cdot\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\mid m\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}2^{-h}\cdot\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\mid m\right]

\displaystyle=2^{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|-h}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}\cdot\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\mid m\right]

\displaystyle=2^{\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|-h}\cdot\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]

∎

6.1.2
Generically Augmenting Security

Definition 34

(Augmented Unclonable Security)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a cloning search game and
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over the message space
\mathcal{M}
. Let
\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
be the following
augmented cloning experiment
, with the modification highlighted in
blue
:

•

Setup Phase:

–

All parties get a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples a message
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

–

\mathcal{R}
sends
\rho
to
\mathcal{A}
.

–

\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
all get oracle access to the point function
P_{m}(\cdot)
.

•

Splitting Phase:

–

\mathcal{A}
computes a bipartite state
\rho^{\prime}
over registers
B,C
.

–

\mathcal{A}
sends
\rho^{\prime}[B]
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\rho^{\prime}[C]
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Challenge Phase:

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples
(r_{\mathcal{B}},r_{\mathcal{C}})\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
and computes
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}(\mathsf{sk},m;r_{\mathcal{B}}),\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}(\mathsf{sk},m;r_{\mathcal{C}})

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

–

\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
send back answers
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes bits
b_{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}})
and
b_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}})
.

–

The outcome of the game is denoted by
\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))
, which equals 1 if
b_{\mathcal{B}}=b_{\mathcal{C}}=1
, indicating that the adversary has won, and 0 otherwise, indicating that the adversary has lost.

Note that
\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
defined the same as
\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
, but
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
additionally get oracle access to
P_{m}(\cdot)
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon,\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})
augmented unclonable security
if for all QPT cloning adversaries
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D},\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}})+\varepsilon(\lambda).

Lemma 27
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a cloning search game with message space
\mathcal{M}
such that
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}})
is negligible. If
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},|\mathcal{M}|^{-\delta})
unclonable search security for some
\delta>0
, then
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
augmented unclonable security.

Proof.

We will define a sequence of hybrids:

Hybrid 1:

This is the original augmented cloning experiment
\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a QPT adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which succeeds with non-negligible probability
p
.

Hybrid 2:

In this hybrid, we replace the oracles
P_{m}(\cdot)
with
P_{S}(\cdot)
, where
S\subset\mathcal{M}
is a random subset containing
m
of size
|\mathcal{M}|^{1-\delta/2}
. The fact that
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}})
is negligible implies that
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}
is negligible, hence
\frac{|S|}{|\mathcal{M}|}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|^{\delta/2}}
is also negligible. We claim that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in this hybrid with probability at least
p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

Suppose not, we will construct an adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
which will violate
Corollary

2
:

•

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
picks
m\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}
and receives oracle access to
\mathcal{O}
, where either
\mathcal{O}=P_{m}
or
\mathcal{O}=P_{S}
for a random subset
S\subset\mathcal{M}
of size
|\mathcal{M}|^{1-\delta/2}
containing
m
.

•

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
then simulates
\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
for
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
, using
m
as the message and using
\mathcal{O}
as the oracle given to
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
. Since
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
are QPT algorithms,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
only makes polynomially many queries.
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs the bit
\mathfrak{ACE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))
.

If
\mathcal{O}=P_{m}
, then the view of
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
is exactly Hybrid 1, and otherwise it is exactly Hybrid 2. Thus,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
has a non-negligible advantage.

Hybrid 3:

In this hybrid, we change the order of sampling. A random subset
S\subset\mathcal{M}
of size
|\mathcal{M}|^{1-\delta/2}
is sampled at the beginning of the experiment, and the message later is sampled as
m\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{S}
. This is perfectly indistinguishable from Hybrid 2, as the view of
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
is unchanged.

Hybrid 4:

In this hybrid, we fix a particular
S
such that
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
succeeds with probability
p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
in Hybrid 3, by an averaging argument.

Now we observe that Hybrid 4 is exactly the experiment
\mathfrak{CE}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{S}}
. Note that the distribution
\mathcal{U}_{S}
has min-entropy
(1-\delta/2)\log_{2}|\mathcal{M}|
. Thus, by
Lemma

26
, we have

\displaystyle p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\leq\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{S}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq|\mathcal{M}|^{\delta/2}\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq|\mathcal{M}|^{-\delta/2},

which contradicts the assumption that
p
is non-negligible.
∎

6.1.3
From Augmented Security to Unclonable-Indistinguishability

Now, we state our result that shows how to go from a search game in the plain model to a search game in QROM with unclonable indistinguishable security. We first invoke
Lemma

27
to generically obtain augmented unclonable-search security and we then leverage this notion of security to obtain unclonable-indistinguishable security.

Theorem 28

(Search to Indistinguishability)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a statistically correct cloning search game with message space
\mathcal{M}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{\ell}
, where
\ell=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
, such that
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}})
is negligible and
\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
does not depend
36
36

36

This requirement can be lifted by extending the definition of stateful cloning games and having
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
know the random coins of
\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}
(in this case
m
). We keep the syntax simple for there is no known application to the more general case.
on
m
. Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},|\mathcal{M}|^{-\delta})
unclonable search security for some
\delta>0
. Let
n=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
, and define a cloning search game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
in QROM as follows:

•

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has message space
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
.

•

Let
H:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be a random oracle.

•

\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda})
runs
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
. It outputs
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=\mathsf{sk}
.

•

\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{sk},m)
. It samples
m\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}
and computes
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(sk,m)
, then it outputs the token
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\otimes\ket{m^{\prime}\oplus H(m)}\!\!\bra{m^{\prime}\oplus H(m)}
and random coins
r_{\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}}=m

•

\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=\mathsf{sk}
. It computes
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
(recall that by assumption this does not require knowledge of
m
) and outputs
\mathsf{ch}
.

•

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a search game, which defines
\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}
.

Then,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has statistical correctness and
\mathsf{negl}
unclonable indistinguishable security.

Proof.

Statistical correctness of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
follows easily from statistical correctness of
\mathcal{G}
, so it suffices to show
\mathsf{negl}
unclonable indistinguishable security. Keep in mind that by
Lemma

27
,
\mathcal{G}
has
\mathsf{negl}
augmented unclonable security. Suppose there exists a QPT cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which breaks the unclonable distinguishing security of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Let
(m_{0}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime})
be the messages used by
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
. Note that
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}})\geq 1/2-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
by the statistical correctness of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
and
Lemma

24
, so that we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\geq\frac{1}{2}+\mu(\lambda)

for some non-negligible function
\mu
.

We first define a sequence of hybrids:

•

Hybrid 0:
The original cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}_{m_{0}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime}}}
.
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
succeeds with probability
1/2+\mu(\lambda)
in this experiment.

•

Hybrid 1:
In this hybrid, we will replace the oracle
H
for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
only with the reprogrammed oracle
H_{1}
, where

\displaystyle H_{1}(x):=\begin{cases}u,\quad x=m\\
H(x),\quad x\neq m\end{cases},

where
u\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
is an independent random string. We claim that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in this hybrid with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}+\mu(\lambda)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

Suppose not, we will construct an adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that breaks the message hiding property of
\mathcal{G}
(
Lemma

25
):

–

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
simulates Hybrid 1 for
\mathcal{A}
, using the token
\rho
it receives and a fresh random oracle
H^{\prime}
it simulates on-the-fly.

–

Then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
measures a random query made by
\mathcal{A}
to
H^{\prime}
.

Note that if
H^{\prime}
was replaced by
H
, the view of
\mathcal{A}
would be exactly Hybrid 0. Thus, by
Theorem

4
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs the message
m
with non-negligible probability, which is larger than
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}})+|\mathcal{M}|^{-\delta}
, a contradiction.

•

Hybrid 2:
In this hybrid, we change the order of sampling with regard to
H(m)
in the experiment without changing the view of
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
, so that the probability of success remains the same. More specifically, we sample
r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and send it to
\mathcal{A}
instead of
m^{\prime}\oplus H(m)
. Then, we sample
b\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}
and give both
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
the reprogrammed oracle
H_{2}^{b}
, where

\displaystyle H_{2}^{b}(x):=\begin{cases}m_{b}^{\prime}\oplus r,\quad x=m\\
H(x),\quad x\neq m\end{cases}

For fixed
\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}
and fixed
H_{-m}
, which is the partial random oracle defined on inputs
x\neq m
, consider the following projectors:

•

\Pi_{B}^{b}:
Run
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
on challenge
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
with oracle
H_{2}^{b}
. Check if the output of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is
m_{b}^{\prime}
. Undo the computation.

•

Similarly define
\Pi_{C}^{b}
for
b\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.

Without loss of generality assume that the bipartite state output by
\mathcal{A}
is a pure state
\ket{\phi_{BC}}
in Hybrid 2. Let
\Pi_{B}=(\Pi_{B}^{0}+\Pi_{B}^{1})/2
and
\Pi_{C}=(\Pi_{C}^{0}+\Pi_{C}^{1})/2
. We spectrally decompose the state as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{BC}}=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C}.

where
\Pi_{B}\ket{\varphi_{i}}=\lambda_{i}\ket{\varphi_{i}}
and
\Pi_{C}\ket{\psi_{j}}=\gamma_{j}\ket{\psi_{j}}
.

Claim 3
.

For any polynomial
p(\cdot)
, with overwhelming probability over
\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}},H_{-m}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\;:\;|\lambda_{i}-1/2|\geq 1/p(\lambda)\\
j\;:\;|\gamma_{j}-1/2|\geq 1/p(\lambda)\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

(14)

Proof.

Assume the quantity in
eq.

14
is a non-negligible function
w(\lambda)
. We will construct an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
which breaks the
\mathsf{negl}
augmented security of
\mathcal{G}
:

•

\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
get oracle access to
P_{m}(\cdot)
, where
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

•

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
gets a quantum token
\rho
. It samples a
2t
-wise independent hash function
H:\left\{0,1\right\}^{\ell}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
to simulate a random oracle and a random string
r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
, where
t=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
is an upper-bound on the number of total random oracle queries made by
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
. Then, it runs
\mathcal{A}
on
\rho\otimes\ket{r}\!\!\bra{r}
to get a bipartite state
\ket{\phi_{BC}}
. It sends this state to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. It also sends the description of
H
to both of them.

•

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
receives a challenge
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
from the challenger. It implements the operator
\Pi_{B}
defined above. Note that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
can reprogram the random oracle
H_{2}
on input
m
using and its oracle access to
P_{m}(\cdot)
. Then,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
applies the efficient symmetric approximate threshold measurement
\mathsf{SATI}_{(P,Q),\gamma}^{\epsilon,\delta}
in
Theorem

9
with
P=(\Pi^{B}_{0}+\Pi^{B}_{1})/2
,
Q=I-P
,
\gamma=3/4p
,
\epsilon=1/4p
and
\delta=2^{-\lambda}
. If the outcome is 0,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
aborts. If the outcome is 1,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
then runs
\mathcal{B}
on the leftover state with
H_{2}^{0}
or
H_{2}^{1}
picked uniformly at random. It measures and outputs a random query
\mathcal{B}
makes to the random oracle.

•

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is defined the same way as
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, in the end outputting a random query made by
\mathcal{C}
to the random oracle.

By
Theorem

9
bullet (1), both
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
will get outcome 1 with non-negligible probability
w-2\delta
, in which case by bullet (2) the leftover state will be
4\delta
-close to the the following state:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:|\lambda_{i}-1/2|>1/4p\\
j:|\gamma_{j}-1/2|>1/4p\end{subarray}}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C}.

Observe that when
\mathcal{B}
does not query
m
, it will succeed with probability exactly
1/2
. Therefore, by
Theorem

4
, the query weight of
\mathcal{B}
on
m
is non-negligible. Similarly, the query weight of
\mathcal{C}
on
m
is non-negligible. Therefore,
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
succeed with non-negligible probability, a contradiction.

∎

Therefore, for any polynomial
p
, we have that
\ket{\phi_{BC}}
is negligibly close to the state
\ket{\phi_{B}}+\ket{\phi_{C}}
, where

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{B}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:|\lambda_{i}-1/2|\leq 1/p\end{subarray}}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C},\quad\ket{\phi_{C}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:|\lambda_{i}-1/2|>1/p\\
j:|\gamma_{j}-1/2|\leq 1/p\end{subarray}}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C},

in which case we could bound the success probability of
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
in Hybrid 2 as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\mu(\lambda)-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\leq(\left|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})(\ket{\phi_{B}}+\ket{\phi_{C}})\right|^{2}+\left|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})(\ket{\phi_{B}}+\ket{\phi_{C})}\right|^{2})/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})|\phi_{B}\rangle+\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})|\phi_{B}\rangle+\langle\phi_{C}|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle+\langle\phi_{C}|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathsf{Re}\left(\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle+\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes I)|\phi_{B}\rangle+\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes I)|\phi_{B}\rangle+\langle\phi_{C}|(I\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle+\langle\phi_{C}|(I\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathsf{Re}\left(\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle+\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi_{B}\otimes I)|\phi_{B}\rangle+\langle\phi_{C}|(I\otimes\Pi_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle+\mathsf{Re}\left(\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{0}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{0}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle+\langle\phi_{B}|(\Pi^{1}_{B}\otimes\Pi^{1}_{C})|\phi_{C}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{p},

where in the last step we used
Lemma

6
. Since
p
is arbitrary, this is a contradiction.

∎

As a corollary, we achieve unclonable encryption in QROM using BB84 states. This is an improvement over the main result of
[
AKL+22
]
, as it can be more easily implemented on near-term quantum computers.

Corollary 8

(Existence of Unclonable Encryption in QROM Using Prepare-and-Measure States)
.

There exists a public-key unclonable encryption scheme with unclonable indistinguishable security in QROM, which uses only prepare-and-measure quantum operations.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84}}
from
Definition

21
, then it satisfies the condition of
Theorem

28
, so there exists
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as described in the theorem. Observe that since
\mathcal{G}
is a cloning encryption game, so is
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Thus,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
gives a construction of unclonable encryption in QROM, and the
\mathsf{negl}
unclonable distinguishing security implies unclonable security of this encryption scheme.

Finally, we invoke the generic compiler in
[
AK21
]
to achieve a public-key scheme
37
37

37

Although
[
AK21
]
only showed that their transformation preserves weak unclonable security, it could easily be checked that it also preserves strong unclonable security.
.
∎

6.2
Indistinguishablity-to-Search

Theorem 29
.

If
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
is a cloning search game with statistical correctness and
\varepsilon
unclonable indistinguishable security, then
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},2\varepsilon+\mathsf{negl})
unclonable security for any distribution
\mathcal{D}
over the message space
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{D}
-evasive.

Proof.

We will give a generic proof that works for an arbitrary extension
\widetilde{\mathsf{GenC}}
, which we will omit for simplicity. Suppose there exists a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which breaks
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security of
\mathcal{G}
. We will construct an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
, which will break
(\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m},\varepsilon^{\prime})
unclonable security of
\mathcal{G}
, where
\varepsilon^{\prime}=\varepsilon/2-\mathsf{negl}
,
m_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
is a fixed message, and
m
is a message sampled as
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
. By convexity, this would imply that there exists a fixed value of
m
for which the security is broken, hence finishing the proof. We describe
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
below:

•

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is the same as
\mathcal{A}
.

•

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
runs
\mathcal{B}
with the input it receives. If the output is
m
,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
outputs
m
; otherwise
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
outputs
m_{0}
.

•

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is defined similarly to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

Note that if the message in the experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m}}
above is
m
, then by assumption
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
wins with probability
p>\varepsilon
. On the other hand, if the message is
m_{0}
, then the probability that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
outputs
m
is at most
\mathsf{OPT}(m\mid\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}})\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
by
Lemma

24
. Hence, by union bound,
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
succeeds with overwhelming probability in this case, and we have

\displaystyle\E_{m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}}\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right))\right]=\frac{1}{2}\left(p+1-\mathsf{negl}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon(\lambda)}{2}-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)

∎

as desired.

7
From Search to Decision Games

We present a transformation from search games to decision games. We remark that the transformation is tailored to application of copy-protection and thus, the resulting decision game has a specific form.

Theorem 30

(Search to Decision)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be a statistically correct cloning encryption game with message space
\mathcal{M}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{\ell}
, where
\ell=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
, such that

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
outputs a uniformly random key
\mathsf{sk}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{K}
, where
|\mathcal{K}|^{-1}
is negligible in
\lambda
.

•

\mathcal{G}
is
\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}
-evasive.

•

\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},|\mathcal{M}|^{-\delta})
unclonable search security for some
\delta>0

Define a cloning decision game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
in QROM as follows:

•

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be an arbitrary message space.

•

Let
H:\mathcal{M}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and
G:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{K}
be random oracles, where
n=\Omega(\lambda)
.

•

\mathsf{Setup}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda})
outputs
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=\bot

•

\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime})
samples
m\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}
. It computes
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(G(m^{\prime}),m)
. It outputs
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\otimes\ket{H(m)}\!\!\bra{H(m)}
.

•

\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime})
is an algorithm which does not depend on
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}
, and outputs a value
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}(\mathsf{sk}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\mathsf{ch}^{\prime},\mathsf{ans}^{\prime})
accepts if
\mathsf{ans}^{\prime}=[\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}==m^{\prime}]
.

Then,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has statistical correctness and
(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathsf{negl})
independent-challenge unclonable security for any unlearnable distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

We begin with correctness. Following
Definition

3
, we define a QPT algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime},\mathsf{ch}^{\prime})
as follows:

•

Parse the token as
\rho^{\prime}=\rho\otimes\ket{y}\!\!\bra{y}
, i.e. measure the last register to obtain
y\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(G(\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}),m)
, recall that this does not require the knowledge of
m
.

•

Compute
m\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})
.

•

Output
b=[H(m)==y]
.

To analyze correctness, we consider two cases: (1)
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}=m^{\prime}
and (2)
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}
.

•

If
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}=m^{\prime}
, then by statistical correctness of
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
receives the correct message
m=\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
above and outputs 1 with overwhelming probability.

•

If
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
sees a challenge generated by a random key
\tilde{\mathsf{sk}}=G(\mathsf{ch}^{\prime})
that is independent from
G(m^{\prime})
, which is the key used to generate the token
\mathcal{A}_{G}
receives. We will show that the probability that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
outputs the correct message
m
in this case is negligible. This will then imply that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
outputs 0 with overwhelming probability, since the output length of
H
is
\Omega(\lambda)
.

Now suppose that
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
outputs the correct message above with non-negligible probability
p
, we will construct an adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that breaks the message hiding property of
\mathcal{G}
using
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}
:

–

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
receives a token
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
, where
\mathsf{sk}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{K}
and
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
then samples
\tilde{\mathsf{sk}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{K}
and computes
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(\mathsf{sk},m)
. Recall that this does not require the knowledge of
m
.

–

Next,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
computes and outputs
\tilde{m}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{G}}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})
.

By assumption,
\tilde{m}=m
with non-negligible probability, since for
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}\neq m^{\prime}
the value
G(\mathsf{ch}^{\prime})
is identically distributed as the value
\tilde{sk}
sampled by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
above. Therefore,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
breaks the message-hiding property of
\mathcal{G}
given in
Lemma

25
, a contradiction.

Next, we show
(\mathcal{D},\mathsf{negl})
unclonable security. Note that by
Lemma

27
,
\mathcal{G}
has
\mathsf{negl}
augmented unclonable security. We will define a sequence of hybrids:

•

Hybrid 0:
This is the original cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{ind}}
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which succeeds in this hybrid with probability
p=p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})+\mu(\lambda)
for a non-negligible function
\mu
.

•

Hybrid 1:
In this hybrid, we will change the oracle
G
for
\mathcal{A}
only to the punctured oracle
G_{m}
, defined as

\displaystyle G_{m^{\prime}}(x):=\begin{cases}u,\quad x=m^{\prime}\\
G(x),\quad x\neq m^{\prime}\end{cases},

where
u\in\mathcal{K}
is an independent uniformly random value. We claim that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeed in this experiment with probability
p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Suppose this is false. Let
\rho_{BC}^{j}
be the bipartite state outputs by
\mathcal{A}
in Hybrid
j
, then
\mathsf{TD}\left(\rho_{BC}^{1},\rho_{BC}^{2}\right)
must be non-negligible, since the only difference between Hybrids 0-1 is on the random oracle
G
for
\mathcal{A}
. Using
\mathcal{A}
, we will construct an adversary that breaks the unlearnability of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
:

–

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
gets oracle access to
P_{m^{\prime}}(\cdot)
(which will not be used), where
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. It samples random oracles
G^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{K}
and
H^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
, as well as a message
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

Then,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
samples
\mathsf{sk}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{K}
, computes
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
, and runs
\mathcal{A}(\rho,H^{\prime}(m))
.

–

During the last step, it measures and outputs a random query made by
\mathcal{A}
to the oracle
G^{\prime}
.

As the view of
\mathcal{A}
in Hybrid 1 is perfectly simulated, by
Theorem

4
, the query weight of
\mathcal{A}
on
m^{\prime}
is non-negligible, hence
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
correctly outputs
m^{\prime}
with non-negligible probability, breaking unlearnability.

•

Hybrid 2:
In this hybrid, we change the order of sampling. A random value
k\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{K}}
is sampled at the beginning of the experiment and the quantum part of the token received by
\mathcal{A}
is calculated as
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(k,m)
. In turn, the random oracle
G
for
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
is replaced by the reprogrammed oracle
G^{2}_{m^{\prime}}
which is defined as

\displaystyle G^{2}_{m^{\prime}}(x):=\begin{cases}k,\quad x=m^{\prime}\\
G(x),\quad x\neq m^{\prime}\end{cases},

This hybrid is perfectly indistinguishable from Hybrid 1, hence
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in this hybrid with probability at least
p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

•

Hybrid 3:
In this hybrid, we replace the oracle
H
for
\mathcal{A}
with the punctured oracle
H_{m}
defined as

\displaystyle H_{m}(x):=\begin{cases}w,\quad x=m\\
G(x),\quad x\neq m\end{cases},

where
w\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
is an independent uniformly random value. We claim that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in this hybrid with probability at least
p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Suppose not, then we have
\mathsf{TD}\left(\rho_{BC}^{2},\rho_{BC}^{3}\right)
is non-negligible. We will use
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
to construct an adversary
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
to break the message hiding property of
\mathcal{G}
:

–

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
receives
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
from the challenger, where
\mathsf{sk}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathcal{K}
and
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

Then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
samples
w\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
and simulates random oracles
H^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{n},G^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{K}
and runs
\mathcal{A}
on input
\rho\otimes\ket{w}\!\!\bra{w}
with oracles
H^{\prime},G^{\prime}
. It measures and outputs a random query made by
\mathcal{A}
to the oracle
H^{\prime}
.

As the view of
\mathcal{A}
in Hybrid 3 is perfectly simulated, by
Theorem

4
, the query weight of
\mathcal{A}
on
m
is non-negligible, hence
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
outputs
m
with non-negligible probability, contradicting the message-hiding property of
\mathcal{G}
given in
Lemma

25
.
