Therefore, we have established that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in Hybrid 3 with probability
p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})+\eta(\lambda)
for some non-negligible function
\eta
. Without loss of generality, assume that the bipartite state created by
\mathcal{A}
is a pure state, i.e.
\rho_{BC}^{3}=\ket{\phi_{BC}}\!\!\bra{\phi_{BC}}
. Consider the following binary POVM elements:

•

\Pi_{B}
: Sample
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}^{\prime}(sk^{\prime},m^{\prime})
. Run
\mathcal{B}
with oracles
H,G^{2}_{m^{\prime}}
and input
\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}
. Measure if the output of
\mathcal{B}
equals
[\mathsf{ch}^{\prime}==m^{\prime}]
, in which case we will say that
\mathcal{B}

passed

\Pi_{B}
.

•

Similarly define
\Pi_{C}
for
\mathcal{C}
.

We write the state received by
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
in spectral decomposition as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{BC}}\!\!\bra{\phi_{BC}}=\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i,j}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C},

where
\Pi_{B}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}=\lambda_{i}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}
and
\Pi_{C}\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C}=\gamma_{j}\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C}
. Let
q
be a polynomial. Let
p^{\mathsf{triv}}:=p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\mathcal{D}^{\prime})
.

We can bound the probability that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in Hybrid 3 as

\displaystyle p-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)\leq\braket{\phi_{BC}|(\Pi_{B}\otimes\Pi_{C})|\phi_{BC}}=\sum_{i,j}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:\;\lambda_{i}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\\
j:\;\gamma_{j}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle+\left(p^{\mathsf{triv}}+\frac{1}{q}\right)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:\;\lambda_{i}\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\\
j:\;\gamma_{j}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}+\left(p^{\mathsf{triv}}+\frac{1}{q}\right)\sum_{i,j:\;\mu_{j}\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}+\frac{1}{q}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:\;\lambda_{i}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\\
j:\;\gamma_{j}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{j}.

Thus, it suffices to show the following claim to reach a contradiction:

Claim 4
.

For any polynomial
q
, with overwhelming probability over
\mathsf{sk}^{\prime}=m,m^{\prime},H,G,u,k,w,
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:\;\lambda_{i}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\\
j:\;\gamma_{j}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/q\end{subarray}}\left|\alpha_{i,j}\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}\gamma_{j}\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda).

Proof.

Suppose that there exists
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which violates
4
. Consider the following cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)
against the
(\mathcal{D},\mathsf{negl})
augmented security of
\mathcal{G}
:

•

\left(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)
get a quantum token
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(k,m)
from the challenger, where
k\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{K}}
and
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
. Also,
\left(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)
get oracle access to
P_{m}(\cdot)
.

•

\mathcal{A}_{1}
samples
38
38

38

Recall that this can be efficiently done via
2t
-wise independent hash functions where
t=\mathsf{poly}(\lambda)
is a query-bound for
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
.
random oracles
G^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{K}
and
H^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
, as well as random strings
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}^{\prime},w\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
. It runs
\mathcal{A}
on input
\rho\otimes\ket{w}\!\!\bra{w}
with oracles
G^{\prime},H^{\prime}
to obtain a bipartite state
\rho_{BC}
, which it sends to
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. In addition,
\mathcal{A}_{1}
sends
(G^{\prime},H^{\prime},m^{\prime},w)
to both
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
.

•

In the challenge phase,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
receives
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}=k\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}(k,m)
from the challenger and
(G^{\prime},\allowbreak H^{\prime},\allowbreak m^{\prime},\allowbreak w,\allowbreak\rho_{BC}[B])
from
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Observe that
\mathcal{B}_{1}
can implement
\Pi_{B}
using these values. In particular, it can reprogram
H^{\prime}
so that it outputs
w
on input
m
using its oracle access to
P_{m}(\cdot)
, and it can reprogram
G^{\prime}
to output
k
. With this in mind,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
applies the efficient approximated threshold measurement
{\sf ATI}_{(P,Q),\gamma_{1}}^{\epsilon,\delta}
in
Theorem

8

with
P=\Pi_{B},Q=I-\Pi_{B},\gamma_{1}=p^{\mathsf{triv}}+3/4q,\epsilon=1/4q
, and
\delta=2^{-\lambda}
, with outcome
b_{B}
. If
b_{B}=0
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
aborts. If
b_{B}=1
, then
\mathcal{B}_{1}
runs
\Pi_{B}
and measures and outputs a random query made by
\mathcal{B}
to the reprogrammed oracle
H^{\prime}
.
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is defined similarly to
\mathcal{B}_{1}
.

By
Theorem

8
bullets (1) and (2), with non-negligible probability the bipartite state obtained by
(\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1})
right before measuring the queries is negligibly close to a state of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i:\;\lambda_{i}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/4q\\
j:\;\gamma_{j}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/4q\end{subarray}}\beta_{i,j}\ket{\varphi_{i}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\psi_{j}}_{C}.

(15)

Before we analyze the success probability of
\left(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{1}\right)
, we define two algorithms
\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is defined the same as
\mathcal{B}_{1}
except it does not reprogram the oracle
H^{\prime}
when implementing
\Pi_{B}
, and similarly for
\mathcal{C}_{2}
. We will refer to this different implementation of
\Pi_{B}
(hence a different operator) as
\Pi_{B}^{\prime}
. We emphasize that the only difference between
(\mathcal{B}_{1},\Pi_{B})
and
(\mathcal{B}_{2},\Pi_{B}^{\prime})
(similarly between
(\mathcal{C}_{1},\Pi_{C})
and
(\mathcal{C}_{2},\Pi_{C}^{\prime})
) is the output of the oracle
H^{\prime}
given to
\mathcal{B}
(
\mathcal{C}
) on input
m
.

In contrast to
eq.

15
, if we consider
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2},\mathcal{C}_{1})
instead, the state shared by
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
at the same stage will have negligible weight on eigenstates of
\Pi_{B}^{\prime}
with eigenvalue
\lambda^{\prime}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/8q
, for otherwise one could violate the message hiding property (
Lemma

25
) using
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
by simulating the view of
\mathcal{B}
without the knowledge of
m
, which is information theoretically hidden from
\mathcal{B}
. Similarly, the state will have negligible weight on eigenstates of
\Pi_{C}^{\prime}
with eigenvalue
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}>p^{\mathsf{triv}}+1/8q
if we consider
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2})
instead. Therefore, By
Theorem

4
, conditioned on
eq.

15
, i.e. conditioned on
b_{B}=b_{C}=1
,
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}
will both output
m
with non-negligible probability, hence breaking
(\mathcal{D},\mathsf{negl})
augmented security of
\mathcal{G}
.

∎

∎

Corollary 9
.

There exists a copy-protection scheme for an arbitrary class of point functions secure in QROM.

Proof.

Follows easily from
Theorem

30
after setting
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
to be the class of point functions represented by the special input
m^{\prime}
, that is,
m^{\prime}
is interpreted as the description of the point function
P_{m^{\prime}}(\cdot)
.
∎

8
Asymmetric Cloning Games

Recall that we defined cloning games (e.g. see
Definition

9
) such that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are required to pass the same verification phase, i.e. with respect to the same algorithms
(\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
. This does not capture all unclonable primitives, in particular cryptography with certified deletion. In this section, we extend further the cloning game syntax to include such primitives and give a simpler proof of an existing feasibility result as an application of our framework in the asymmetric setting.

8.1
Definitions

We start with the formal definition of asymmetric cloning games.

Definition 35

(Asymmetric Cloning Game)
.

An
asymmetric cloning game
consists of a tuple of efficient algorithms
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}})
:

•

Key Generation:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
is a PPT algorithm which takes as input a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
in unary. It outputs a secret key
\mathsf{sk}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. We will assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{sk}
always contains the security parameter
1^{\lambda}
in order to simplify the notation below.

•

Token Generation:

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
is a QPT algorithm takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m\in\{0,1\}^{*}
. It outputs a quantum token
\rho
.

•

Challenge Generation:

\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
and a message
m
. It outputs a classical challenge
\mathsf{ch}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
.
\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}}
has the same syntax.

•

Verification:

\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})
takes as input a secret key
\mathsf{sk}
, a message
m
, a challenge
\mathsf{ch}
, and an answer
\mathsf{ans}
. It outputs either
0
(reject) or
1
(accept).
\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}
has the same syntax.

Correctness.

For correctness, we require that the verifications for
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are both individually doable given the entire quantum token.

Definition 36

(Correctness of Asymmetric Cloning Games)
.

Let
\delta_{\mathcal{B}},\delta_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to[0,1]
and
\delta=(\delta_{\mathcal{B}},\delta_{\mathcal{C}})
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
has
\delta
-correctness if there exist efficient quantum algorithms
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{G}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}
such that for all messages
m\in\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})=1\end{subarray}\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{ans}\leftarrow\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{G}}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})\end{subarray}\right]\geq\delta_{\mathcal{B}}(\lambda),\quad\Pr\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})=1\end{subarray}\;:\;\begin{subarray}{c}\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})\\
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{ch}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},m)\\
\mathsf{ans}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}}(\rho,\mathsf{ch})\end{subarray}\right]\geq\delta_{\mathcal{C}}(\lambda).

If
\delta=(1,1)
(or
\delta(\lambda)=(1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda),1-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda))
), we say
\mathcal{G}
has
perfect
(or
statistical
) correctness.

Security.

We define security formally so that
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are asked to pass their corresponding verifications. We highlight the part different from
Definition

6
in
blue
.

Definition 37

(Asymmetric Cloning Experiment)
.

An
asymmetric cloning experiment
, denoted by
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
, is a security game played between a referee
\mathsf{Ref}
and a cloning adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
. It is parameterized by an asymmetric cloning game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}})
and a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over the message space
\mathcal{M}
. The experiment is described as follows:

•

Setup Phase:

–

All parties get a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples a message
m\leftarrow\mathcal{D}
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes
\mathsf{sk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\rho
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•

Splitting Phase:

–

\mathcal{A}
computes a bipartite state
\rho^{\prime}
over registers
B,C
.

–

\mathcal{A}
sends
\rho^{\prime}[B]
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\rho^{\prime}[C]
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

Challenge Phase:

–

\mathsf{Ref}
samples
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
sends
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

–

\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
send back answers
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}}
, respectively.

–

\mathsf{Ref}
computes bits
b_{\mathcal{B}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}})
and
b_{\mathcal{C}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}})
.

–

The outcome of the game is denoted by
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))
, which equals 1 if
b_{\mathcal{B}}=b_{\mathcal{C}}=1
, indicating that the adversary has won, and 0 otherwise, indicating that the adversary has lost.

Definition 38

(Asymmetric Trivial Cloning Attack)
.

We say that
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
is a
\mathcal{B}
-trivial cloning attack
against an asymmetric cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
if
\mathcal{A}
upon receiving a token
\rho
, sends the product state
\ket{\bot}\!\!\bra{\bot}\otimes\rho
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
. In other words, only
\mathcal{C}
gets the token
\rho
. We denote by
\mathsf{TRIV}{{}_{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
the set of
\mathcal{B}
-trivial attacks against
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
. We similarly define
\mathsf{TRIV}{{}_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
as the set of
{\mathcal{C}}
-trivial attacks.

We denote by
\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
the set of trivial attacks against
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
.

Finally, we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}):=\mathsf{TRIV}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})\cup\mathsf{TRIV}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})

As pointed out in
Remark

2
, the definition above captures mixtures of
\mathcal{B}
-trivial and
\mathcal{C}
-trivial attacks by convexity.

Definition 39

(Asymmetric Trivial Success Probability for Cloning Games)
.

We define the
\mathcal{B}
-trivial success probability of an asymmetric cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}):=\sup_{\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)\in\mathsf{TRIV}{{}_{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})}\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right].

We similarly define
p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})
as the
\mathcal{C}
-trivial success probability of
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
. Accordingly, we define the trivial success probability of
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}
as

\displaystyle p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}):=

\displaystyle\sup_{\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)\in\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})}\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max\left(p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}),p^{\mathsf{triv}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})\right),

where the last equality follows from the fact that any
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)\in\mathsf{TRIV}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
is a convex combination of attacks from
\mathsf{TRIV}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
and
\mathsf{TRIV}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}})
.

Definition 40

(Asymmetric Cloning Search Game)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathsf{GenT},\mathsf{GenC},\mathsf{Ver})
be an asymmetric cloning game such that
\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},m,\mathsf{ch},\mathsf{ans})
accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}=m
. Then,
\mathcal{G}
is called an
asymmetric cloning search game
.

Remark 9
.

In the definition above, the "search" restriction only applies to
\mathcal{C}
. As such, the definition complements the asymmetry between
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, while being consistent with
Definition

4
.

With the modified definitions of trivial success, cloning experiment and search game, the notions of unclonable security for asymmetric games are defined similarly to
Definitions

9
,
10
and
11
.

Definition 41

((Asymmetric) Unclonable Security)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an asymmetric cloning game,
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution over the message space
\mathcal{M}
, and
\varepsilon:\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to[0,1]
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\epsilon)
unclonable security
if for all QPT cloning adversaries
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
we have:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq p^{\mathsf{triv}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})+\varepsilon(\lambda).

If
|\mathcal{M}|=1
, we will simply write
\varepsilon
unclonable security.

Definition 42

((Asymmetric) Unclonable Search Security)
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is an asymmetric cloning search game with
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable security, we additionally say that
\mathcal{G}
has
(\mathcal{D},\varepsilon)
unclonable search security
.

Definition 43

((Asymmetric) Unclonable Indistinguishable Security)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m_{1}}
denote the distribution that outputs messages
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
with probability
1/2
each. We say that an asymmetric cloning search game
\mathcal{G}
has
\varepsilon
unclonable indistinguishable security
if it has
(\mathcal{D}_{m_{0},m_{1}},\varepsilon)
unclonable search security for any pair of messages
m_{0},m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}
.

Remark 10
.

Note that
Definition

43
could have significantly different flavor compared to its symmetric counterpart (
Definition

11
), given that the distinguishing between
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
need not be simultaneous.

Remark 11

(Extended Asymmetric Cloning Games)
.

One could consider the extended definition for asymmetric cloning games with correlated challenge distributions as in
Section

4.4
, but it is unnecessary for the certified deletion setting which we will focus on next.

Applications.

Asymmetric cloning games provide a framework to analyze unclonable primitives in which the goal of the adversary is to perform two different tasks (as
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
), each of which require a quantum token, using only one copy of the token. Examples include primitives with certified deletion property, analyzed below in
Section

8.2
, where
\mathcal{C}
is asked to perform the intended use of the primitive and
\mathcal{B}
is asked to generate a classical certificate of deletion for the quantum token. Yet another example is secure software leasing
[
AL20
]
, where
\mathcal{B}
is asked to return a quantum (software) token and
\mathcal{C}
is asked to achieve the functionality of the software.

Tokenized Signatures.

Another class of unclonable primitives is
one-time primitives
, in which an honest user consumes the quantum token after using it once. While it is possible to model a one-time primitive as a (regular) cloning game, it is more natural to cast it as an asymmetric cloning game. We will demonstrate this using the example of
tokenized signatures
for single-bit messages. Informally, in a tokenized signature scheme, a quantum signing token
\rho
can be used to sign one (and only one) bit
b\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. Formally, a tokenized signature scheme
[
BS16
,
CLLZ21
,
Shm22
]
is a tuple of efficient algorithms
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
:

•

\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
takes as input a security parameter and outputs a pair of classical keys
(vk,sk)
.

•

\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(sk)
takes as input a secret key and outputs a quantum signature token
\rho
.

•

\mathsf{Sign}(\rho,x)
takes as input a token
\rho
and a classical message
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. It outputs a classical signature
\sigma_{x}
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}(vk,x,\sigma)
takes as input a verification key, a classical message, and a classical signature. It outputs 0 (reject) or 1 (accept).

(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
defines an asymmetric cloning game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{TS}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}})
, as follows:

•

Similar to quantum money, there is no message, i.e.
m=\bot
.

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
runs
(vk,sk)\leftarrow\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and outputs
\mathsf{sk}=(vk,sk)
.

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(vk,sk)
, computes
\rho\leftarrow\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime}(sk)
and outputs
\rho\otimes\ket{vk}\!\!\bra{vk}
.

•

There is no challenge for either
\mathcal{B}
or
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.
\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}}
always output
\bot
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathsf{sk},\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ans})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(vk,sk)
. It computes
b\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}(vk,0,\mathsf{ans})
and accepts if
b=1
.

•

\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},\bot,\bot,\mathsf{ans})
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(vk,sk)
. It computes
b\leftarrow\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime}(vk,1,\mathsf{ans})
and accepts if
b=1
.

Informally,
\mathcal{B}
is signing the bit
0
and
\mathcal{C}
is signing the bit
1
using the signing token.

Correctness.

We say that the tokenized signature scheme
(\mathsf{Gen},\mathsf{GenT}^{\prime},\mathsf{Sign},\mathsf{Ver}^{\prime})
satisfies correctness if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{TS}}
has perfect correctness.

Security.

We say that the scheme has unclonable security if
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{TS}}
has
(\mathcal{D}_{\bot},\varepsilon)
unclonable security, where
\varepsilon=\mathsf{negl}
yields optimal security.

8.2
Deletion Games

Certified deletion can be defined as a special case of asymmetric cloning games, where
\mathcal{B}
gets no challenge.

Definition 44

(Deletion Game)
.

A
deletion game
is an asymmetric cloning game
\mathcal{G}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}})
such that
\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}}
always outputs
\bot
.

Note that in the definition above,
\mathcal{B}
represents
\mathcal{A}
generating a classical certificate for deleting
\rho
. Since
\mathcal{B}
gets no challenge,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
can be effectively considered as one party.

Unclonable Encryption with Certified Deletion.

A notable primitive in this category is unclonable encryption with certified deletion, which we can define as a deletion game
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{UE-CD}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}})
with the following properties:

•

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{UE-CD}}
is an asymmetric cloning search game.

•

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{UE-CD}}
has statistical correctness.

•

\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathsf{sk},m)
outputs
\mathsf{sk}
with probability 1.

For this primitive, like regular unclonable encryption, we consider two types of security: (1)
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},\varepsilon)
unclonable security and
\varepsilon
unclonable indistinguishable security, the latter of which is stronger.

8.3
Construction of Unclonable Encryption with Certified Deletion

8.3.1
Preliminaries

We cite two lemmas from literature that we will need in our construction. The first lemma is commonly used to bound the value of monogamy-of-entanglement games.

Lemma 31

(Lemma 2 in
[
TFKW13
]
)
.

Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{N}
be positive-semidefinite operators over a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
and let
\left\{\pi_{k}\right\}_{k\in[N]}
be
N
mutually orthogonal permutations over
[N]
. Then,

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i\in[N]}A_{i}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}\leq\sum_{k\in[N]}\max_{i\in[N]}\left\|\sqrt{A_{i}}\sqrt{A_{\pi^{k}(i)}}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}},

where
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}
denotes the operator norm, also known as the Schatten-
\infty
norm.

The second lemma we will need is the local version of quantum Goldreich-Levin (
Lemma

23
, which was known in previous work
[
AC02
,
CLLZ21
]
.

Lemma 32

(Quantum Goldreich-Levin)
.

Suppose a quantum algorithm
\mathcal{A}
, given a quantum state
\rho
, a key
k
, and a random string
r\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
can output
\langle r,x\rangle(\pmod{2})
with probability
1/2+\varepsilon
. Then, there exists a quantum algorithm (extractor)
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, which, given the same quantum state
\rho
and the key
k
, can output
x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
with probability
4\varepsilon^{2}
.

8.3.2
Achieving Unclonable Search Security

We first describe a known construction
39
39

39

This is a simplified version of the construction of
[
BI20
]
.
based on BB84 states, denoted as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}=(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}})
:

•

The message space is
\mathcal{M}=\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
outputs
\mathsf{sk}=\theta\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\theta,m)
takes as input
\theta,m\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
and outputs
\rho=\ket{m^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{m^{\theta}}
, where
\ket{m^{\theta}}=H^{\theta}\ket{m}

•

\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}}(\theta,m)
outputs
\bot
as required by a deletion game.

•

\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}}(\theta,m,\bot,\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}})
accepts if and only if
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B}}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
satisfies
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{B},i}=m_{i}
for all
i\in\theta[1]
, where
\theta[b]:=\left\{i\in[n]:\theta_{i}=b\right\}
for
b\in\left\{0,1\right\}
.

•

\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}}(\theta,m)
outputs
\theta
as required by unclonable encryption with certified deletion.

•

\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}(\theta,m,\mathsf{ch}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}})
checks if
\mathsf{ans}_{\mathcal{C}}=m
as required by an unclonable search game.

Correctness.

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
satisfies perfect correctness. In order to generate a certificate,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}}(\rho,\bot)
measures
\rho
in the Fourier basis and outputs the result, whereas in order to decrypt,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}}(\rho,\theta)
computes
H^{\theta}\rho H^{\theta}
, then measures in the computational basis and outputs the result.

Security.

While it is possible to show that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
above satisfies
\mathsf{negl}
unclonable indistinguishable security, the proofs involve either entropic arguments
[
BI20
]
or other advanced techniques
[
BK22
]
. We will instead follow a different approach which we believe is simpler in many aspects. First, we will show that
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
satisfies
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
unclonable security by reduction to a monogamy-of-entanglement game following the techniques of
[
TFKW13
]
, similar to the first construction of unclonable encryption
[
BL20
]
. Then, we will modify the scheme and apply Quantum Goldreich-Levin to achieve
\mathsf{negl}
unclonable indistinguishable security. We give the formal details below.

Theorem 33
.

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
defined above has
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},\mathsf{negl})
information theoretic unclonable search security.

Proof.

We first define a monogamy-of-entanglement game
\mathsf{MOE}=\mathsf{MOE}(\lambda)
for certified deletion which is closely related to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
. Let
\theta[b]
be defined as above.

\mathsf{MOE}
is a game between a referee
\mathsf{Ref}
and an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
:

•

\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
and
\mathsf{Ref}
get a security parameter
1^{\lambda}
as input.

•

\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
prepare a bipartite state
\rho_{XA}
and send
\rho[X]
(the
X
register) to
\mathsf{ch}
, where
X=\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

•

\mathcal{A}
computes a bipartite state
\rho^{\prime}
over registers
B,C
, then sends
\rho^{\prime}[B]
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\rho^{\prime}[C]
to
\mathcal{C}
.

•

\mathsf{Ref}
samples
\theta\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
and measures the
X
register in the basis
\left\{\ket{x^{\theta}}\right\}_{x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}
, obtaining outcome
x
.

•

\mathcal{B}
outputs
x_{B}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}

•

\mathcal{C}
gets
\theta
as input and outputs
x_{C}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
.

•

The outcome of the game is denoted by
\mathsf{MOE}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))
, which equals 1 if
x_{C}=x
and
x_{B,i}=x_{i}
for all
i\in\theta[1]
, indicating that the adversary has won, and 0 otherwise, indicating that the adversary has lost.

We will begin with showing that the success probability of any adversary in this game is exponentially small. The proof leverages the widely used techniques of
[
TFKW13
]
.

Claim 5
.

For any (unbounded) adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{MOE}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt[4]{2}}\right)^{\lambda}

Proof.

We can write the winning probability of
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
as follows:

\displaystyle p_{\text{win}}:=\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{MOE}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{\theta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\forall i\in\theta[1]\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right)\rho\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\forall i\in\theta[1]\end{subarray}}\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}},

where
\left\{B_{x^{\prime}}\right\}_{x^{\prime}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}
, as well as
\left\{C^{\theta}_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}
for any
\theta\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
, is a POVM. By a standard purification argument, we can w.l.o.g. assume that the POVM’s are projective measurements. Next, we can apply
Lemma

31
to get

\displaystyle p_{\text{win}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}\sum_{k\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}\max_{\theta\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}\left\|\Pi^{\theta}\Pi^{\pi_{k}(\theta)}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}},

(16)

where

\displaystyle\Pi^{\theta}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in\theta[1]\end{subarray}}\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x},

and
\pi_{k}:\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}\to\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
are
2^{\lambda}
mutually orthogonal permutations to be determined later. For convenience, we define the index sets
S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]:=\left\{i\in[\lambda]:\theta_{i}=\theta_{i}^{\prime}\right\}
(’same’ indices) and
D[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]:=\left\{i\in[\lambda]:\theta_{i}\neq\theta_{i}^{\prime}\right\}
(’different’ indices). Also let
\theta^{\prime}=\pi_{j}(\theta)
and
s=|S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]|
for short-hand notation. Now, we have

\displaystyle\Pi^{\theta^{\prime}}\leq\bar{Q}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta^{\prime}}[1]\end{subarray}}\ket{x^{\theta^{\prime}}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta^{\prime}}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes I,

hence

\displaystyle\left\|\Pi^{\theta}\Pi^{\theta^{\prime}}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}\leq\left\|\Pi^{\theta}\bar{Q}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}^{2}=\left\|\Pi^{\theta}\bar{Q}\Pi^{\theta}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,z,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},z_{i}^{\prime}=z_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta}[1]\\
y_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i},\;\forall i\in\theta^{\prime}[1]\end{subarray}}\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\cdot\ket{y^{\theta^{\prime}}}\!\!\bra{y^{\theta^{\prime}}}\cdot\ket{z^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{z^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}B_{y^{\prime}}B_{z^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}C^{\theta}_{z}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta}[1]\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i},\;\forall i\in\theta^{\prime}[1]\end{subarray}}\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\cdot\ket{y^{\theta^{\prime}}}\!\!\bra{y^{\theta^{\prime}}}\cdot\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta}[1]\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i},\;\forall i\in\theta^{\prime}[1]\end{subarray}}\left|\braket{x^{\theta}|y^{\theta^{\prime}}}\right|^{2}\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}

(17)

\displaystyle=2^{s-\lambda}\left\|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta}[1]\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=y_{i},\;\forall i\in\theta^{\prime}[1]\\
x_{i}=y_{i},\;\forall i\in S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]\end{subarray}}\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}

(18)

Above in
eq.

17
we used the fact that the inner product
\left|\braket{x^{\theta}|y^{\theta^{\prime}}}\right|
vanishes unless
x_{i}=y_{i}
for all
i\in S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]
, in which case it is contributed a factor of
2^{-1/2}
for every index
i\notin S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]
. Now in
eq.

18
, every term in the sum with distinct
(x,x^{\prime})
is orthogonal, so we only need to count the number of
y
values for given
(x,x^{\prime})
. Specifically, we need the number of
y\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
such that
y_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\theta^{\prime}[1]
and
y_{i}=x_{i}
for
i\in S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]
. Note that these two conditions never contradict because
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i}
for all
i\in\theta[1]
, and
S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]\cap\theta^{\prime}[1]\subseteq\theta[1]
. Hence,
y
has
|S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]\cup\theta^{\prime}[1]|
coordinates fixed. The number of free coordinates, then is given by
\lambda-|S[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]\cap\theta^{\prime}[1]|=|\theta[1]\setminus\theta^{\prime}[1]|
, which is the number of indices where
\theta_{i}^{\prime}=0,\theta_{i}=1
. Thus,
eq.

18
equals
2^{-t}
, where
t
is the number of indices
i
such that
\theta_{i}=0,\theta^{\prime}_{i}=1
, then we can bound
eq.

16
as

\displaystyle p_{\text{win}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}\sum_{k\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}\max_{\theta\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}\left\|\Pi^{\theta}\Pi^{\pi_{k}(\theta)}\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}\sum_{k\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}\max_{\theta\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}2^{-t/2}

Furthermore, observe that by symmetry, we can achieve the same bound for
t
representing the number of indices
i
with
\theta_{i}=1,\theta_{i}^{\prime}=0
as well for any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
. Thus, without loss of generality we could assume
t\geq|D[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]|/2
by choosing the optimal order in the analysis. Finally, we choose
\pi_{k}(\theta)=\theta\oplus k
to be the cyclic permutations, which yields
\binom{\lambda}{k}
permutations with
D[\theta,\theta^{\prime}]=k
. Thus,

\displaystyle p_{\text{win}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{\lambda}}\sum_{k=0}^{\lambda}\binom{\lambda}{k}2^{-k/4}=\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt[4]{2}}\right)^{\lambda}\approx 0.920^{\lambda}

as desired.

∎

Remark 12
.

[
CV21
]
bounds an easier version of
\mathsf{MOE}
by
0.85^{\lambda/2}\approx 0.924^{\lambda}
, where
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
need to guess indices in
\theta[0]
and
\theta[1]
after learning
\theta
, respectively. Because
\theta
is unknown,
\mathcal{A}
cannot trivially succeed by splitting the qubits between them.

We finish the proof of the theorem by giving a reduction from unclonable security of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
to hardness of
\mathsf{MOE}
. Since
0.920^{\lambda}
is negligible in
\lambda
, this suffices.

Claim 6
.

Suppose there exists an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
breaking
(\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}},\varepsilon)
unclonable search security of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}
, then there exists
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
such that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{MOE}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right]>\varepsilon.

Proof.

Define
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
as follows:

•

\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
creates
\lambda
EPR pairs, i.e. the bipartite state
\ket{\psi}=2^{-\lambda/2}\sum_{x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}{\ket{x}\ket{x}}
over registers
XA
, and send the
X
register to the referee
\mathsf{Ref}
.

•

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
applies the same splitting channel as
\mathcal{A}
. Similarly,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
apply the same measurements as
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
.

We will show that
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
defined above succeeds in
\mathsf{MOE}
with probability equal to the success probability of
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
. Recall that for any
\theta\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
, we have
\ket{\psi}=2^{-\lambda/2}\sum_{x\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}{\ket{x^{\theta}}\ket{x^{\theta}}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathsf{MOE}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right))\right]=\E_{\theta}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta^{\prime}}[1]\end{subarray}}\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right)(I_{X}\otimes\mathcal{A})\left(\ket{\psi}\!\!\bra{\psi}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=2^{-\lambda}\E_{\theta}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta^{\prime}}[1]\end{subarray}}\sum_{y,z}\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right)(I_{X}\otimes\mathcal{A})\left(\ket{y^{\theta}}\bra{z^{\theta}}\otimes\ket{y^{\theta}}\bra{z^{\theta}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=2^{-\lambda}\E_{\theta}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta^{\prime}}[1]\end{subarray}}\sum_{y,z}\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right)\left(\ket{y^{\theta}}\bra{z^{\theta}}\otimes\mathcal{A}\left(\ket{y^{\theta}}\bra{z^{\theta}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=2^{-\lambda}\E_{\theta}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta^{\prime}}[1]\end{subarray}}\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right)\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\bra{x^{\theta}}\otimes\mathcal{A}\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\bra{x^{\theta}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\E_{\theta,x}\mathsf{Tr}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,x^{\prime}:\\
x_{i}^{\prime}=x_{i},\;\forall i\in{\theta^{\prime}}[1]\end{subarray}}\left(B_{x^{\prime}}\otimes C^{\theta}_{x}\right)\left(\mathcal{A}\left(\ket{x^{\theta}}\bra{x^{\theta}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[1\leftarrow\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}},\mathcal{U}_{\mathcal{M}}}(1^{\lambda},\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right))\right],

which completes the proof.

∎

∎

8.3.3
Achieving Unclonable-Indistinguishable Security

Now, we give the construction of unclonable-indistinguishable secure unclonable encryption with certified deletion, denoted as
\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}^{\mathsf{UI}}=((\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenT},\allowbreak\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{B}},\allowbreak\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathsf{GenC}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathsf{Ver}_{\mathcal{C}}))
below:

•

We consider single-bit messages, i.e.
\mathcal{M}=\left\{0,1\right\}

•

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
samples
\theta\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
and
r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}\setminus\{0^{\lambda}\}
independently. It outputs
\mathsf{sk}=(\theta,r)
.

•

\mathsf{GenT}(\mathsf{sk},m)
parses the input as
\mathsf{sk}=(\theta,r)
. It samples
x\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{x^{\prime}\in\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}\;:\;\langle r,x^{\prime}\rangle=m\right\}
, where we treat
\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
as
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{\lambda}
for the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. It outputs
\rho=\ket{x^{\theta}}\!\!\bra{x^{\theta}}
.

Theorem 34
.

\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}^{\mathsf{UI}}
above is an unclonable encryption scheme for single-bit messages with certified deletion, which has
\mathsf{negl}
information theoretic unclonable indistinguishable security.

Proof.

Correctness is easy to see. For security, we define a sequence of hybrids:

•

Hybrid 0:
The asymmetric cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}}^{\mathsf{UI}},\;\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}}}
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists an adversary
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
which succeeds in this experiment with probability
1/2+\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon
is non-negligible in
\lambda
.

•

Hybrid 2:
In this hybrid, we sample
r\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
, i.e. we allow
r=0^{\lambda}
.

Since the probability of this is negligibly small, the success probability of
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
in this Hybrid is
\varepsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
.

•

Hybrid 2:
In this hybrid, instead of sampling
m
uniformly and sampling
x
conditioned on
\langle r,x\rangle=m
, we sample
x\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
uniformly and set
m=\langle r,x\rangle
. In other words, we remove
m
from the experiment and ask
\mathcal{C}
to output
\langle r,x\rangle
in order to pass verification.

This hybrid is statistically indistinguishable from Hybrid 0, since
\left|\Pr\left[\langle r,x\rangle=0\right]-1/2\right|\leq\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
for
r,x\xleftarrow{\$}\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}
. Thus,
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
succeeds in this Hybrid with probability
\varepsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
. Let
p_{\mathcal{B}}
be the probability that
\mathcal{B}
passes verification, and let
p_{\mathcal{C}}
be the probability that
\mathcal{C}
passes verification conditioned on
\mathcal{B}
passing verification. Then,
p_{\mathcal{B}}p_{\mathcal{C}}\geq\varepsilon-\mathsf{negl}(\lambda)
, so that
p_{\mathcal{B}}
and
p_{\mathcal{C}}
are both non-negligible in
\lambda
.

•

Hybrid 3:
This is the asymmetric cloning experiment
\mathfrak{CE}_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathsf{BB84-CD}},\;\mathcal{U}_{\left\{0,1\right\}^{\lambda}}}
.

Observe that the only difference between Hybrids 2 and 3 is the verification phase for
\mathcal{C}
. Accordingly, we define an adversary
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
for this Hybrid, where
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is the extractor guaranteed by
Lemma

32
, applied with respect to the mixed state received by
\mathcal{C}
in Hybrid 1 conditioned on
\mathcal{B}
passing verification. The guarantee of
Lemma

32
states that if
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is the probability of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
passing verifiction conditioned on
\mathcal{B}
passing verification, then
p_{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\geq 4p_{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
, which is non-negligible. Therefore,
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
succeeds in Hybrid 3 with non-negligible probability
p_{\mathcal{B}}p_{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
, contradicting
Theorem

33
.

∎

Remark 13
.

For simplicity, we give a construction for single-bit messages, but a standard hybrid argument can be used to show that bitwise encryption works for multi-bit messages in the certified deletion setting.

Appendix A
Alternate Proof of Simultaneous Quantum Goldreich-Levin

Below we give an alternate
40
40

40

Kundu and Tan have independently generalized the Goldreich-Levin technique to the non-local (simultaneous) setting
[
KT22
]
. The authors apply this technique to achieve a weaker form of unclonable encrpytion in the plain model, whereas we apply it to achieve single-decryptor encryption with unclonable security against independently generated ciphertexts.
proof of
Lemma

23
which does not use the lifting theorem.

Direct proof of
Lemma

23
.

We will adapt the proof
41
41

41

See Lemma B.12 in
[
CLLZ21
]
.
of
[
CLLZ21
]
, originally due to
[
AC02
]
, to the simultaneous case. We can assume that the token
\rho_{k,x}=\ket{\psi_{k,x}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{k,x}}
is a pure state, for the mixed state case follows by convexity. Observe that we can defer any measurements made by
\left(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\right)
until the very end. Accordingly, we can model
\mathcal{A}
as a unitary map
\Phi
which acts as:

\displaystyle\Phi\ket{\psi_{k,x}}\ket{0^{m}}_{aux}=\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC},

where
\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}
is a bipartite state shared by
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
. In phase 2, the key
k
as well as the random coins
(r,r^{\prime})
are revealed and unitary maps
(U^{k,r}_{B},U^{k,r^{\prime}}_{C})
are applied by
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
, respectively. The resulting state then is given by

\displaystyle\left(U^{k_{B},r}_{B}\otimes U^{k_{C},r^{\prime}}_{C}\right)\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\langle r,x\rangle}_{B}\ket{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{0}}_{BC}+\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\langle r,x\rangle}_{B}\ket{\overline{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{1}}_{BC}

\displaystyle+\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\overline{\langle r,x\rangle}}_{B}\ket{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{2}}_{BC}+\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\overline{\langle r,x\rangle}}_{B}\ket{\overline{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{3}}_{BC}\bigg)

\displaystyle=:\ket{\Gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}},

where
\ket{\phi^{j}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}
is a normalized state for
j\in\left\{0,1,2,3\right\}
and
\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}
is the coefficient corresponding to the case of the adversary succeeding, so that we can express the assumption as

\displaystyle\E_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\right|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon

and hence

\displaystyle\E_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\left|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\right|^{2}-\left|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\right|^{2}-\left|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\right|^{2}+\left|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\right|^{2}\geq\E_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\left(2\left|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\right|^{2}-1\right)\geq 2\varepsilon

(19)

We now describe the new adversary
\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\right)
:

•

Given
\ket{\psi_{k,x}}
in phase 1,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
acts the same as
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. it applies
\Phi
, obtaining the state
\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}
.

•

After receiving the key
k
from the challenger (ignoring the random coins received),
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
prepares a uniform superposition over
r\in\mathcal{R}
and applies the unitary
U^{k_{B}}_{B}
, where we define
U^{k_{E}}_{E}
as
U^{k_{E}}_{E}\ket{r}\ket{\varphi}=\ket{r}U^{k_{E},r}_{E}\ket{\varphi}
for
E\in\left\{B,C\right\}
. Then,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
applies a
Z
gate to the register storing the inner product
\langle r,x\rangle
, and applies
(U^{k_{B}}_{B})^{\dagger}
to its state. Finally,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
measures the register storing the random coins
r
in the Fourier basis and outputs the result.

•

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is defined in a similar fashion.

Next, we will analyze the evolution of the state shared by
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
step by step. Since the actions of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
commute, we can synchronously track their operations. After the first step, the state is given by

\displaystyle\left(U^{k_{B}}_{B}\otimes U^{k_{C}}_{C}\right)\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}=\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}\ket{\Gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}.

Next,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
each apply a
Z
gate to their register storing the inner product, which results in the state

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}(-1)^{\langle r,x\rangle\oplus\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}\bigg(\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\langle r,x\rangle}_{B}\ket{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{0}}_{BC}

\displaystyle-\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\langle r,x\rangle}_{B}\ket{\overline{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{1}}_{BC}-\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\overline{\langle r,x\rangle}}_{B}\ket{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{2}}_{BC}

\displaystyle+\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\ket{\overline{\langle r,x\rangle}}_{B}\ket{\overline{\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}}_{C}\ket{\phi_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}^{3}}_{BC}\bigg)

\displaystyle=:\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}\ket{\Gamma^{\prime}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}},

with

\displaystyle\langle\Gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}\rangle=(-1)^{\langle r,x\rangle\oplus\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}\left(|\alpha_{k,x,r}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r}|^{2}-|\theta_{k,x,r}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r}|^{2}\right).

Now
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
uncompute the unitary
U^{k_{B}}_{B}\otimes U^{k_{C}}_{C}
, and the state becomes

\displaystyle\left(U^{k_{B}}_{B}\otimes U^{k_{C}}_{C}\right)^{\dagger}\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}\ket{\Gamma^{\prime}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}=\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}\left(U^{{k_{B}},r}_{B}\otimes U^{{k_{C}},r^{\prime}}_{C}\right)^{\dagger}\ket{\Gamma^{\prime}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\ket{r}_{B}\ket{r^{\prime}}_{C}\left((-1)^{\langle r,x\rangle\oplus\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}\left(|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}\right)\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}+\ket{\mathsf{err}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}\right),

where
\ket{\mathsf{err}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}
is a subnormalized state orthogonal to
\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}
.

Next,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
each apply a Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) on their random coins, resulting in the state

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{R}|^{2}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\sum_{y,z\in\mathcal{R}}(-1)^{\langle r,y\rangle\oplus\langle r^{\prime},z\rangle}\ket{y}_{B}\ket{z}_{C}\bigg((-1)^{\langle r,x\rangle\oplus\langle r^{\prime},x\rangle}\big(|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}

\displaystyle-|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}\big)\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}_{BC}+\ket{\mathsf{err}_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}}\bigg).

Note that the coefficient of
\ket{x}_{B}\ket{x}_{C}\ket{\varphi_{k,x}}
equals

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|^{2}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2},

so the probability that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
both output
x
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\Pr\left[y=z=x\right]

\displaystyle\geq\E_{k,x}\left|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|^{2}}\sum_{r,r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\E_{k,x}\left|\E_{r,r^{\prime}}|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left|\E_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|\alpha_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\beta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}-|\theta_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}+|\gamma_{k,x,r,r^{\prime}}|^{2}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 4\varepsilon^{2},

where we used Cauchy-Schwartz Inequality and
eq.

19
.

∎
